Below is a scenario-driven set of **observability interview questions with answers**, including **logs, metrics, tracing, correlation IDs, OpenTelemetry, Prometheus, Grafana, Loki/ELK, alerts, resilience, Kubernetes, and debugging distributed transactions**.

## 1\. What is observability?

**Interview answer:**

Observability is the ability to understand the internal state of a distributed system from its external outputs.

The three primary pillars are:

- **Logs** — what happened?
- **Metrics** — how much/how often?
- **Traces** — where did the request spend time and where did it fail?

For example:

```
Client
  |
  v
API Gateway
  |
  +----> Product Service
  |          |
  |          +----> PostgreSQL
  |
  +----> Inventory Service
             |
             +----> PostgreSQL
```

If checkout takes 5 seconds:

- Metrics tell us latency increased.
- Logs tell us an exception occurred.
- Traces tell us that `InventoryService` spent 4.5 seconds waiting for its database.

---

# 2\. Scenario: API is suddenly slow

### Question

Your `/orders` API normally takes 200 ms but suddenly takes 5 seconds. How would you investigate?

### Strong answer

I would investigate from the outside in:

```
1. Check API latency metrics
2. Check error rate
3. Check distributed traces
4. Identify slow downstream service
5. Check service logs using correlation/trace ID
6. Check database/cache/external API metrics
7. Check infrastructure metrics
```

For example:

```
POST /orders
        |
        | 120ms
        v
Order Service
        |
        | 80ms
        v
Product Service
        |
        | 4.7 seconds
        v
Inventory Service
        |
        v
PostgreSQL
```

The trace immediately tells me the problem isn't necessarily the Order Service itself.

---

# 3\. Scenario: One request crosses five microservices

Suppose:

```
Frontend
   |
API Gateway
   |
Order Service
   |
Product Service
   |
Inventory Service
   |
Payment Service
```

### Question

How do you identify which service failed?

Use **distributed tracing**.

A request receives:

```
traceId = abc123
```

Each service creates spans:

```
Trace: abc123

Gateway
 └── OrderService
      ├── ProductService
      ├── InventoryService
      └── PaymentService
```

Every log generated during that request should contain:

```
traceId
spanId
service.name
timestamp
level
message
```

Then you can search all logs for:

```
traceId=abc123
```

---

# 4\. What is the difference between traceId and spanId?

This is a very common interview question.

### Trace ID

Identifies the **entire request journey**.

```
traceId = ABC123
```

### Span ID

Identifies one operation within that trace.

```
Trace ABC123

Gateway       span=001
OrderService  span=002
Inventory     span=003
Database      span=004
```

So:

```
traceId = entire request
spanId  = individual operation
```

---

# 5\. What is a correlation ID?

A correlation ID is an identifier used to correlate logs belonging to the same business/request flow.

For example:

```
X-Correlation-Id: 8ef8e4ef-c141-4eb6-80bf-ddbd31aa82fa
```

Your logs could look like:

```
OrderService:
corrId=abc123 Creating order

ProductService:
corrId=abc123 Fetching product

InventoryService:
corrId=abc123 Checking stock

PaymentService:
corrId=abc123 Processing payment
```

You can then search:

```
corrId=abc123
```

and reconstruct the request flow.

---

# 6\. Correlation ID vs Trace ID

This distinction is important.

| Correlation ID | Trace ID |
| --- | --- |
| Application-level identifier | Distributed tracing identifier |
| Can be manually generated | Usually generated by tracing system |
| Useful for log correlation | Used for trace/span relationships |
| Simple | More structured |
| Can exist without tracing | Normally part of tracing |

Modern systems often propagate both.

```
X-Correlation-Id
traceparent
```

---

# 7\. Scenario: Request enters Gateway but correlation ID disappears

### Question

You see:

```
Gateway:
corrId=123

Order Service:
corrId=123

Inventory Service:
corrId=null
```

What is wrong?

The correlation ID is not being propagated from Order Service to Inventory Service.

The HTTP client should propagate:

```
X-Correlation-Id: 123
```

Similarly, tracing context should propagate:

```
traceparent: ...
```

For Spring applications, this is commonly handled through Micrometer/OpenTelemetry instrumentation rather than manually copying headers everywhere.

---

# 8\. What is Micrometer?

Micrometer is essentially the instrumentation/metrics facade commonly used by Spring Boot applications.

It provides APIs for recording:

```
Counter
Gauge
Timer
DistributionSummary
```

Example:

```
Counter.builder("orders.created")
        .register(meterRegistry)
        .increment();
```

Spring Boot Actuator exposes metrics.

For example:

```
/actuator/metrics
```

Prometheus can scrape metrics through:

```
/actuator/prometheus
```

---

# 9\. What is Prometheus?

Prometheus is a **metrics collection and time-series database**.

Typical architecture:

```
Spring Boot
    |
    | /actuator/prometheus
    v
Prometheus
    |
    v
Grafana
```

Prometheus periodically scrapes the application.

Example:

```
http_server_requests_seconds_count
http_server_requests_seconds_sum
jvm_memory_used_bytes
process_cpu_usage
```

---

# 10\. What is Grafana?

Grafana is primarily used for:

- dashboards
- visualization
- querying metrics
- alert visualization
- operational monitoring

For example:

```
                    Grafana
                       |
          +------------+-------------+
          |            |             |
       CPU Usage   HTTP Latency   Error Rate
```

---

# 11\. Scenario: CPU suddenly reaches 100%

### Question

What would you check?

First:

```
CPU metric
```

Then:

```
Which pod/container?
Which service?
Which endpoint?
```

Then application metrics:

```
request rate
latency
error rate
thread count
GC activity
```

Then JVM:

```
heap usage
GC pause
thread count
deadlocks
```

Then traces/logs.

Potential causes:

- infinite loop
- excessive traffic
- inefficient algorithm
- excessive serialization
- database query problem
- GC pressure
- thread contention

---

# 12\. Scenario: Memory keeps increasing

You observe:

```
Heap:
500 MB
600 MB
700 MB
800 MB
900 MB
```

but it doesn't come down.

Possible causes:

- memory leak
- unbounded cache
- static collection
- excessive object retention
- thread-local misuse
- large response buffering
- incorrect caching

I would check:

```
JVM heap metrics
GC metrics
heap dump
allocation profiling
```

---

# 13\. Scenario: High GC

### Question

Your service is experiencing high GC pauses. What do you investigate?

Check:

```
heap usage
old-gen usage
GC frequency
GC pause duration
allocation rate
```

Then determine whether the application is:

```
creating too many objects
```

or

```
retaining too many objects
```

Don't immediately increase heap size.

Increasing:

```
-Xmx
```

may only hide the underlying problem.

---

# 14\. Scenario: Error rate suddenly increases

Your dashboard shows:

```
Requests: 1000/sec
5xx: 300/sec
```

What next?

Break down errors by:

```
service
endpoint
HTTP status
exception
dependency
```

For example:

```
POST /orders

500 -> 20%
404 -> 2%
401 -> 1%
```

Then inspect traces for 500 responses.

You may discover:

```
Order Service
      |
      v
Inventory Service
      |
      v
DB connection pool exhausted
```

---

# 15\. What metrics should every microservice expose?

At minimum:

### HTTP

```
request count
request rate
response status
latency
```

### JVM

```
heap
non-heap
GC
threads
CPU
```

### Database

```
connection pool
active connections
idle connections
connection acquisition time
```

### Business

```
orders.created
payments.success
payments.failed
inventory.out_of_stock
```

Business metrics are extremely valuable.

---

# 16\. Technical metrics vs business metrics

Technical metric:

```
http.server.requests
```

Business metric:

```
orders.created
orders.cancelled
payments.failed
inventory.out_of_stock
```

Suppose:

```
HTTP error rate = 0%
```

but:

```
payment.failure.rate = 30%
```

Your infrastructure may look healthy while your business is actually failing.

---

# 17\. Scenario: HTTP is healthy but customers complain

This is a classic observability question.

Dashboard:

```
HTTP 200 = 99.9%
CPU = 40%
Memory = 50%
```

Yet customers say checkout isn't working.

What could happen?

Your application might return:

```
200 OK
```

with:

```
{
  "success": false
}
```

Therefore infrastructure metrics won't detect the business failure.

Solution:

Add business metrics:

```
checkout.success
checkout.failure
payment.success
payment.failure
order.created
```

---

# 18\. What is RED monitoring?

RED is especially useful for APIs.

### R — Rate

Requests per second.

```
1000 req/sec
```

### E — Errors

Error rate.

```
5xx = 2%
```

### D — Duration

Latency.

```
p50 = 100ms
p95 = 300ms
p99 = 1.2s
```

---

# 19\. What is USE monitoring?

USE is commonly used for infrastructure/resources.

### U — Utilization

```
CPU = 80%
```

### S — Saturation

```
thread pool queue = 500
```

### E — Errors

```
network errors
disk errors
connection errors
```

RED is great for services.

USE is great for infrastructure/resources.

---

# 20\. Why is p99 latency more important than average latency?

Suppose:

```
100 requests
99 requests = 50ms
1 request = 10 seconds
```

Average latency might still look reasonable.

But the user experiencing the slow request has a terrible experience.

Therefore monitor:

```
p50
p90
p95
p99
```

For critical APIs, p99 is particularly useful.

---

# 21\. Scenario: Database is slow

Trace:

```
OrderService
   |
   +-- ProductService 100ms
   |
   +-- InventoryService 100ms
   |
   +-- DB 4.8 seconds
```

Then inspect:

```
DB connection pool
slow queries
locks
indexes
query execution plan
number of connections
CPU
I/O
```

A common mistake is blaming the microservice when the actual problem is the database.

---

# 22\. Scenario: Connection pool exhausted

Metrics:

```
active connections = 50
max connections = 50
pending requests = 200
```

Symptoms:

```
latency increases
timeouts
5xx
```

Potential causes:

- slow queries
- transactions staying open
- connection leak
- database unavailable
- too much traffic

Don't simply increase the pool.

If the database supports only 100 connections and you configure:

```
20 pods × 50 connections
```

you could theoretically request:

```
1000 DB connections
```

which can make the situation worse.

---

# 23\. Scenario: Service A calls Service B and B is down

What should happen?

Without resilience:

```
A
|
+----> B
       |
       X DOWN

A waits
A threads consumed
A becomes slow
A eventually fails
```

This can cause cascading failure.

Use:

- timeout
- retry
- circuit breaker
- bulkhead
- fallback

For example:

```
Service A
   |
 timeout 2 sec
   |
Circuit Breaker
   |
Service B
```

---

# 24\. How does observability help circuit breakers?

Suppose:

```
inventory.failure.rate = 70%
```

Circuit breaker changes:

```
CLOSED
   ↓
OPEN
```

Metrics should expose:

```
circuit breaker state
successful calls
failed calls
rejected calls
slow calls
```

Then Grafana can show:

```
Inventory Circuit Breaker: OPEN
```

---

# 25\. Scenario: Retry makes production worse

Suppose Payment Service is down.

Order Service retries:

```
Request
 ↓
Payment
 ↓
failure
 ↓
retry
 ↓
failure
 ↓
retry
 ↓
failure
```

Now 1,000 requests become 3,000 requests.

This can create a **retry storm**.

Use:

```
limited retries
exponential backoff
jitter
timeouts
circuit breakers
```

Observe retry metrics.

---

# 26\. What is distributed tracing?

A trace represents an entire request.

Example:

```
Trace ID: 123

API Gateway
 └── Order Service
      ├── Product Service
      │    └── PostgreSQL
      │
      ├── Inventory Service
      │    └── PostgreSQL
      │
      └── Payment Service
           └── Payment Provider
```

Each operation is a span.

---

# 27\. What is OpenTelemetry?

OpenTelemetry is a vendor-neutral observability framework for collecting and exporting:

```
traces
metrics
logs
```

It can export data to systems such as:

```
Jaeger
Tempo
Zipkin
Prometheus
OTel Collector
```

A common architecture is:

```
Spring Boot
    |
OpenTelemetry
    |
OTel Collector
    |
    +----> Jaeger/Tempo
    |
    +----> Prometheus
    |
    +----> Loki/ELK
```

---

# 28\. What is an OpenTelemetry Collector?

The Collector sits between applications and observability backends.

```
Applications
     |
     v
OTel Collector
     |
     +----> Jaeger
     |
     +----> Tempo
     |
     +----> Prometheus
```

Advantages:

- centralized configuration
- batching
- filtering
- sampling
- retrying exports
- routing telemetry

This prevents every service from being tightly coupled to one observability backend.

---

# 29\. Scenario: Trace exists but logs cannot be correlated

Trace:

```
traceId=abc123
```

Log:

```
2026-10-04 ERROR Payment failed
```

There is no trace ID.

Problem: logging context is not connected to tracing context.

You want structured logs such as:

```
{
  "timestamp": "...",
  "level": "ERROR",
  "service": "payment-service",
  "traceId": "abc123",
  "spanId": "xyz789",
  "message": "Payment failed"
}
```

Then from a trace you can jump directly to relevant logs.

---

# 30\. Structured logging vs plain logging

Bad:

```
Payment failed for order 123
```

Better:

```
{
  "event": "payment_failed",
  "orderId": "123",
  "traceId": "abc",
  "service": "payment-service"
}
```

Structured logging is much easier for systems such as:

```
ELK
OpenSearch
Loki
Splunk
Datadog
```

to search and aggregate.

---

# 31\. What should NOT be logged?

Never casually log:

```
password
JWT token
refresh token
credit card number
CVV
API secret
client secret
personal sensitive data
```

For example, avoid:

```
Authorization: Bearer eyJ...
```

in application logs.

Your earlier HTTP logging configuration includes:

```
include-body: true
```

Be particularly careful with this in production because request bodies can contain credentials or sensitive data.

---

# 32\. Scenario: Production logs are 100 GB/day

What would you do?

First identify:

```
Which service?
Which log level?
Which endpoint?
Which exception?
```

Then:

- remove excessive INFO logging
- prevent duplicate stack traces
- use sampling where appropriate
- configure retention
- rotate logs
- use structured logging
- send logs to centralized storage
- avoid logging entire request/response bodies

---

# 33\. What is log aggregation?

Instead of:

```
Service A -> local file
Service B -> local file
Service C -> local file
```

use:

```
Service A ─┐
Service B ─┼──> Log Collector ──> Loki/Elasticsearch
Service C ─┘
```

Then search from one place.

---

# 34\. ELK stack

ELK means:

```
Elasticsearch
Logstash
Kibana
```

Typical architecture:

```
Application
   |
   v
Logstash
   |
   v
Elasticsearch
   |
   v
Kibana
```

Today you may also see:

```
OpenSearch
Loki
Fluent Bit
Vector
```

depending on the architecture.

---

# 35\. Scenario: Kubernetes pod keeps restarting

How do you investigate?

First:

```
kubectl get pods
```

Then:

```
kubectl describe pod <pod>
```

Check:

```
OOMKilled
CrashLoopBackOff
readiness failure
liveness failure
image issue
configuration issue
```

Then:

```
kubectl logs <pod>
```

and:

```
kubectl logs <pod> --previous
```

The `--previous` command is particularly useful when the container has already crashed.

---

# 36\. Liveness vs readiness

### Liveness

Answers:

> Is the application alive?

If liveness fails repeatedly, Kubernetes may restart the container.

### Readiness

Answers:

> Can this instance receive traffic?

If readiness fails:

```
Pod remains running
but traffic is removed
```

This distinction is very important.

---

# 37\. Scenario: Deployment causes 500 errors

You deploy version:

```
v2
```

and errors increase.

Observability should let you compare:

```
v1 vs v2

error rate
latency
CPU
memory
business metrics
```

If:

```
v1 -> 0.2% errors
v2 -> 12% errors
```

you should consider:

```
rollback
```

or stop the rollout.

This is where canary/blue-green deployment combined with observability becomes powerful.

---

# 38\. What is an SLI?

SLI = **Service Level Indicator**

It is the actual measured value.

Example:

```
Successful requests / total requests
```

Suppose:

```
99,000 successful
1,000 failed
```

SLI:

```
99%
```

---

# 39\. What is SLO?

SLO = **Service Level Objective**

Example:

```
99.9% successful requests
```

The SLI is what you measure.

The SLO is the target.

---

# 40\. What is SLA?

SLA = **Service Level Agreement**

This is generally the contractual/business commitment.

Example:

```
99.9% availability
```

Simplified:

```
SLI = measurement
SLO = target
SLA = contractual commitment
```

---

# 41\. What is an error budget?

Suppose your SLO is:

```
99.9% availability
```

Allowed failure:

```
0.1%
```

That allowed failure is your error budget.

If the service consumes the error budget rapidly:

```
slow down feature releases
prioritize reliability
```

---

# 42\. Scenario: Alert says service is down, but users are fine

Possible problem: bad alert.

For example:

```
CPU > 80%
```

doesn't necessarily mean the service is unavailable.

Better alerts are based on user impact:

```
high 5xx rate
high latency
availability failure
SLO burn rate
```

Avoid alerting on every metric.

---

# 43\. What makes a good alert?

A good alert should answer:

```
What is broken?
Who is affected?
How serious is it?
What should I do?
```

Bad:

```
CPU = 81%
```

Better:

```
Order Service p99 latency > 2 seconds
for 10 minutes
```

Even better:

```
Checkout availability SLO burn rate is critical
```

---

# 44\. Scenario: Inventory service returns empty list

Your API:

```
GET /inventory/api/inventory?productName=iphone_13
```

returns:

```
[]
```

Is that necessarily an error?

No.

It could mean:

```
No inventory record exists
```

This is where business observability matters.

You might add:

```
inventory.lookup.count
inventory.product.not_found
inventory.out_of_stock
```

Then distinguish:

```
service failed
```

from:

```
product has no inventory record
```

---

# 45\. Important issue in your Inventory code

You have:

```
public List<InventoryResponse> isInStock(List<String> productName)
```

but:

```
findByProductNameIn(List<String> skuCode)
```

The parameter is named `skuCode`, but the entity field is:

```
productName
```

and your data loader stores:

```
"Iphone 13"
```

while your request is:

```
productName=iphone_13
```

Those are different values.

Therefore:

```
iphone_13
```

doesn't match:

```
Iphone 13
```

so:

```
[]
```

is expected.

This is a **functional/data-model issue**, not an observability issue.

---

# 46\. Scenario: Order flow fails randomly

Your architecture:

```
                    ┌── Product Service
                    |
Client → Gateway → Order Service
                    |
                    ├── Inventory Service
                    |
                    └── Payment Service
```

One order fails.

Use:

```
traceId
```

to reconstruct:

```
Gateway        20ms
Order          50ms
Product       100ms
Inventory      80ms
Payment      4000ms  <-- problem
```

Then inspect Payment logs:

```
traceId=abc123
```

You may discover:

```
Payment provider timeout
```

Now you have the root cause instead of guessing.

---

# 47\. Scenario: Distributed transaction fails halfway

Suppose:

```
Order created
Inventory reserved
Payment failed
```

You cannot simply use one database transaction across all microservices.

Use a distributed transaction strategy such as:

### Saga

```
Create Order
    ↓
Reserve Inventory
    ↓
Process Payment
```

If payment fails:

```
Cancel Order
Release Inventory
```

Observability should record each state transition:

```
order.created
inventory.reserved
payment.failed
inventory.released
order.cancelled
```

---

# 48\. Scenario: How do you monitor a Saga?

Create business events/metrics:

```
saga.started
saga.completed
saga.failed
saga.compensation.started
saga.compensation.failed
```

Also trace the entire workflow:

```
traceId=abc123

OrderCreated
   ↓
InventoryReserved
   ↓
PaymentFailed
   ↓
InventoryReleased
   ↓
OrderCancelled
```

This is much easier to troubleshoot than individual service logs.

---

# 49\. Scenario: Kafka consumer is falling behind

Important metrics:

```
consumer lag
records consumed
records produced
processing latency
consumer errors
rebalance count
```

If:

```
lag = 1,000,000
```

investigate:

```
consumer throughput
partition count
consumer instances
processing time
downstream dependencies
```

Don't blindly add consumers.

Kafka partitions limit effective parallelism.

---

# 50\. Scenario: API is healthy but Kafka processing is delayed

HTTP metrics:

```
200 OK
latency = 100ms
```

Everything looks fine.

But orders are delayed 10 minutes.

Why?

The asynchronous portion isn't represented by HTTP metrics.

You need:

```
Kafka consumer lag
event processing latency
event age
failed messages
DLQ count
```

This is an excellent interview scenario.

---

# 51\. What is a DLQ?

DLQ = Dead Letter Queue.

Messages that repeatedly fail processing can be moved to a DLQ.

Example:

```
Order Topic
    |
Consumer
    |
    X processing failure
    |
Retry
    |
Retry
    |
DLQ
```

Monitor:

```
dlq.messages.count
```

A growing DLQ should normally trigger an alert.

---

# 52\. Scenario: External payment API is slow

Trace:

```
Order Service
    |
Payment Service
    |
External Payment API
       |
       | 8 sec
```

You should have:

```
timeout
circuit breaker
retry policy
metrics
distributed trace
```

Metrics:

```
payment.external.latency
payment.external.errors
payment.external.timeout
payment.external.success
```

---

# 53\. What is sampling in tracing?

If your system receives:

```
100,000 requests/sec
```

storing every trace may be expensive.

Sampling decides which traces to retain.

Examples:

```
10% normal traffic
100% errors
100% slow requests
```

This is much more useful than blindly storing everything.

---

# 54\. Scenario: Only failed requests should be traced in detail

A good strategy is:

```
Normal request:
sampled

Slow request:
keep

Error:
keep

Critical business operation:
keep
```

This reduces observability cost while preserving valuable debugging information.

---

# 55\. What should your Spring Boot observability architecture look like?

For your type of ecommerce microservices:

```
                         ┌──────────────┐
                         │   Grafana    │
                         └──────▲───────┘
                                |
                         ┌──────┴───────┐
                         │  Prometheus  │
                         └──────▲───────┘
                                |
                         Micrometer
                                |
 ┌──────────┐     ┌────────────┴───────────┐
 │ Gateway  │     │ Spring Boot Services   │
 └────┬─────┘     │                         │
      │           │ Product                 │
      ├──────────>│ Inventory               │
      │           │ Order                   │
      │           │ Payment                 │
      │           │ Cart                    │
      │           └────────────┬────────────┘
      │                        |
      │                  OpenTelemetry
      │                        |
      │                ┌───────▼───────┐
      │                │ OTel Collector│
      │                └───┬───────┬───┘
      │                    │       │
      │                  Tempo    Loki
      │                 /Jaeger   /ELK
      │
      └────────────────────────────────────
```

---

# 56\. What should you expose through Spring Boot Actuator?

Typical endpoints:

```
/actuator/health
/actuator/info
/actuator/metrics
/actuator/prometheus
```

Potentially:

```
/actuator/loggers
```

but secure management endpoints carefully.

Don't expose sensitive actuator endpoints publicly.

---

# 57\. Scenario: Health endpoint returns UP but service doesn't work

This is an important interview trap.

A basic health check may only tell you:

```
JVM is alive
```

but:

```
Database is down
Redis is down
Kafka is unavailable
```

could still exist.

Use appropriate health indicators/readiness checks.

For Kubernetes:

```
liveness
readiness
```

should have different purposes.

---

# 58\. Scenario: Database is down

Should liveness fail?

Usually **not necessarily**.

If you make liveness depend on the database:

```
DB down
 ↓
liveness fails
 ↓
Kubernetes restarts pod
 ↓
DB still down
 ↓
restart
 ↓
restart
```

You can create a restart storm.

Readiness is often more appropriate:

```
DB unavailable
 ↓
readiness fails
 ↓
remove pod from traffic
```

---

# 59\. Scenario: Need to debug one customer's request

You should be able to search using:

```
traceId
correlationId
orderId
userId
```

But be careful with PII.

A useful log structure might be:

```
{
  "service": "order-service",
  "traceId": "abc123",
  "orderId": "ORD-1001",
  "event": "order_created",
  "level": "INFO"
}
```

---

# 60\. Most important observability interview scenario

### Interviewer:

> Production checkout is failing. You have 20 microservices. You cannot reproduce locally. What do you do?

### Strong answer:

I would avoid randomly checking services.

First establish whether this is:

```
availability
latency
functional
dependency
infrastructure
```

Then:

```
1. Check SLO/error-rate dashboard.
2. Identify affected endpoint/service.
3. Find representative failed request.
4. Obtain traceId/correlationId.
5. Inspect distributed trace.
6. Identify failing/slow span.
7. Search centralized logs using traceId.
8. Check downstream dependency metrics.
9. Check DB/cache/message-broker metrics.
10. Determine whether the failure is systemic or isolated.
11. Apply mitigation such as rollback, circuit breaker, scaling or traffic reduction.
12. After mitigation, perform root-cause analysis.
```

That answer demonstrates **production engineering maturity**, rather than just knowledge of tools.

---

# 61\. Design-pattern questions related to observability

You should also expect these.

### Filter / Interceptor pattern

Useful for:

```
correlation ID
request logging
timing
security context
```

### Decorator pattern

Useful for wrapping operations with:

```
metrics
tracing
logging
```

### Observer pattern

Useful conceptually for:

```
events
metrics
listeners
```

### Strategy pattern

Useful for:

```
sampling strategies
retry policies
alerting strategies
```

### Chain of Responsibility

Useful for:

```
HTTP filters
security filters
logging filters
correlation filters
```

### Proxy pattern

Common in:

```
Spring AOP
transactions
metrics
tracing
security
```

---

# 62\. One architecture question you should be ready for

### "Design observability for your ecommerce system."

Answer:

```
                        CLIENT
                          |
                          v
                    API Gateway
                          |
             +------------+-------------+
             |            |             |
             v            v             v
          Product     Inventory       Order
             |            |             |
             +------------+-------------+
                          |
                     PostgreSQL

                          |
                   OpenTelemetry
                          |
                          v
                  OTel Collector
                    /         \
                   /           \
                  v             v
             Trace Store    Log Store
             Tempo/Jaeger   Loki/ELK
                  |
                  v
               Grafana
                  ^
                  |
             Prometheus
                  ^
                  |
              Micrometer
```

Every service should have:

```
structured logs
correlation ID
trace ID
metrics
health checks
business metrics
database metrics
HTTP metrics
```

---

## The 15 questions I would prioritize for your interview

If you have limited preparation time, master these first:

1. **How would you debug a slow API in production?**
2. **How do you trace a request across 10 microservices?**
3. **Trace ID vs Span ID vs Correlation ID?**
4. **How do you propagate correlation/tracing context?**
5. **Micrometer vs Prometheus vs Grafana?**
6. **OpenTelemetry and OTel Collector?**
7. **How do you investigate high CPU?**
8. **How do you investigate memory leaks/GC problems?**
9. **How do you troubleshoot database connection-pool exhaustion?**
10. **How do you detect cascading failures?**
11. **How do retries, timeouts and circuit breakers interact?**
12. **How do you monitor Kafka consumer lag/DLQ?**
13. **How do you design observability for a Saga?**
14. **What are SLI, SLO, SLA and error budget?**
15. **Design an end-to-end observability architecture for an ecommerce platform.**

The strongest interview answers connect **observability + resilience + distributed systems + business impact**, rather than treating logging, metrics, and tracing as isolated tools.


---
This is a strong **top-15 observability/SRE interview set**. If you're preparing for a Java/Spring Boot + microservices role, I'd master them in this order:

## 1\. How would you debug a slow API in production?

A strong answer should follow a systematic path:

1. **Confirm the symptom** — latency p50/p95/p99, error rate, affected endpoints, start time.
2. **Check recent changes** — deployment, configuration, database changes, traffic spike.
3. **Use distributed tracing** — identify which service/span is consuming the time.
4. **Check infrastructure metrics** — CPU, memory, GC, thread pools, connection pools.
5. **Check dependencies** — database, Kafka, Redis, external APIs.
6. **Inspect logs** using the Trace ID.
7. **Profile if necessary** — thread dump, CPU profile, JVM analysis.
8. **Mitigate first** if customer impact is severe, then perform root-cause analysis.

**Interview phrase:**

> "I start with the golden signals—latency, traffic, errors and saturation—then drill down using traces, logs and metrics."

---

## 2\. How do you trace a request across 10 microservices?

Use **distributed tracing**.

Example:

```
Client
  │
  └── API Gateway
       │ trace-id = abc123
       └── Order Service
            ├── Payment Service
            ├── Inventory Service
            └── Shipping Service
```

One **Trace ID** identifies the complete request journey.

Each operation creates a **Span**:

```
Trace: abc123

Gateway       Span A
Order         Span B
Payment       Span C
Inventory     Span D
Database      Span E
```

The trace lets you answer:

> "Where did the 3-second request spend its time?"

---

## 3\. Trace ID vs Span ID vs Correlation ID

| ID | Purpose |
| --- | --- |
| **Trace ID** | Identifies the complete distributed request |
| **Span ID** | Identifies one operation within that trace |
| **Correlation ID** | Application-level identifier used to correlate related logs/events |

Important nuance:

**Correlation ID and Trace ID are not necessarily the same thing.**

Modern systems often use the Trace ID as the primary correlation mechanism, but business workflows may additionally have:

```
orderId
customerId
correlationId
traceId
```

---

## 4\. How do you propagate tracing context?

Typically through HTTP headers using **W3C Trace Context**:

```
traceparent: 00-<trace-id>-<parent-span-id>-01
```

For asynchronous messaging, the context needs to travel with the message.

For example:

```
HTTP Request
    ↓
Order Service
    ↓
Kafka message + trace context
    ↓
Payment Consumer
```

With Spring Boot, Micrometer Tracing/OpenTelemetry instrumentation can handle much of this propagation automatically.

---

## 5\. Micrometer vs Prometheus vs Grafana

This is a common interview trap.

### Micrometer

**Instrumentation/metrics facade.**

Application code produces metrics through Micrometer.

```
Spring Boot
    ↓
Micrometer
```

### Prometheus

**Metrics collection/storage/query system.**

```
Application
   ↓
/actuator/prometheus
   ↓
Prometheus
```

### Grafana

**Visualization and dashboards.**

```
Prometheus ──→ Grafana
```

So:

> **Micrometer instruments → Prometheus collects/stores → Grafana visualizes.**

---

## 6\. What are OpenTelemetry and the OTel Collector?

**OpenTelemetry (OTel)** is a vendor-neutral observability framework for generating and collecting:

- Traces
- Metrics
- Logs

A typical architecture:

```
Applications
     │
     │ OTLP
     ▼
OTel Collector
     │
     ├──→ Prometheus
     ├──→ Jaeger/Tempo
     ├──→ Loki
     └──→ Commercial APM
```

The **Collector** acts as a centralized telemetry pipeline.

It can perform:

- batching
- filtering
- enrichment
- sampling
- transformation
- routing/export

This prevents applications from becoming tightly coupled to a particular observability vendor.

---

## 7\. How do you investigate high CPU?

I'd answer in layers:

### Application level

Check:

- CPU utilization
- thread count
- request rate
- latency
- thread pools

### JVM level

Take thread dumps and identify threads consuming CPU.

Look for:

- infinite/expensive loops
- excessive serialization
- regex problems
- excessive object processing
- lock contention
- inefficient algorithms

Then use a profiler if required.

**Key interview point:**

> Don't restart the application immediately and destroy the evidence unless you're performing an emergency mitigation.

---

## 8\. How do you investigate memory leaks/GC problems?

Start with:

```
Heap usage
   ↓
GC frequency
   ↓
GC pause duration
   ↓
Old-generation occupancy
   ↓
Heap dump
```

Look for:

- objects continuously retained
- unbounded caches
- static collections
- listeners not deregistered
- ThreadLocal misuse
- large collections
- excessive object creation

A useful distinction:

### High allocation rate

Objects are being created rapidly.

### Memory leak

Objects that should be collectible remain reachable.

### GC problem

The JVM spends excessive time reclaiming memory.

Don't automatically equate high memory usage with a memory leak.

---

## 9\. Database connection-pool exhaustion

Typical symptoms:

```
Requests
   ↓
Waiting for DB connection
   ↓
Timeout
   ↓
More retries
   ↓
More load
```

Check:

- active connections
- idle connections
- pending connection requests
- acquisition timeout
- pool size
- query latency
- slow queries
- DB max connections

For HikariCP, for example, monitor:

```
active
idle
pending
max
```

Then determine whether the problem is:

> **Too many requests, slow queries, insufficient pool size, connection leak, or database saturation.**

Simply increasing the pool size can actually make the database problem worse.

---

## 10\. How do you detect cascading failures?

A classic pattern:

```
Service A
   ↓
Service B
   ↓
Service C
   ↓
Database
```

If C becomes slow:

```
C slow
 ↓
B threads blocked
 ↓
B becomes slow
 ↓
A threads blocked
 ↓
A becomes slow
 ↓
Entire system degrades
```

Look for:

- rising latency
- thread-pool exhaustion
- connection-pool exhaustion
- retry storms
- growing queues
- increasing timeouts
- dependency error rates

Prevent it with:

- timeouts
- bounded retries
- exponential backoff + jitter
- circuit breakers
- bulkheads
- rate limiting
- load shedding

---

## 11\. How do retries, timeouts and circuit breakers interact?

This is one of the **highest-value questions**.

Bad design:

```
A retries 3 times
  ↓
B retries 3 times
  ↓
C retries 3 times
```

One user request can create a huge number of downstream requests.

Better:

```
Timeout
   ↓
Limited retry
   ↓
Exponential backoff + jitter
   ↓
Circuit breaker
   ↓
Fallback / fail fast
```

Important:

> Retries should generally happen only for transient failures and should have strict limits.

Also ensure the timeout budget is smaller than the caller's overall deadline.

---

## 12\. How do you monitor Kafka consumer lag/DLQ?

For Kafka consumers, monitor:

- consumer lag
- records consumed
- processing latency
- consumer errors
- rebalance frequency
- partition assignment
- throughput

Conceptually:

```
Produced:
████████████████████

Consumed:
██████████████

              ↑
           lag grows
```

A **DLQ/DLT** should also be monitored:

```
DLQ messages
DLQ growth rate
oldest message age
processing failures
```

Don't just alert on "DLQ contains one message." Alerting should account for **rate, age and business impact**.

---

## 13\. How do you design observability for a Saga?

A Saga is particularly difficult because the transaction is distributed.

I'd propagate:

```
traceId
sagaId
orderId
correlationId
```

Example:

```
Create Order
    │
    ├── Reserve Inventory
    │       ✓
    │
    ├── Charge Payment
    │       ✓
    │
    ├── Ship Order
    │       ✗
    │
    └── Compensate Payment
            ✓
```

Observability should answer:

> What happened to this order, which Saga step failed, and were compensating actions successfully executed?

Track:

- Saga state
- current step
- completed steps
- failed step
- compensation status
- retry count
- event/message IDs
- business identifiers

Distributed tracing alone isn't enough; **business-level state is critical**.

---

## 14\. SLI vs SLO vs SLA vs Error Budget

This should be an automatic answer.

### SLI — Indicator

What you measure.

Example:

> 99.95% of requests completed successfully.

### SLO — Objective

The reliability target.

> 99.9% availability per month.

### SLA — Agreement

A contractual commitment to the customer.

> If availability falls below X, customer receives service credits.

### Error budget

How much unreliability you can tolerate.

If SLO = **99.9%**, the error budget is:

```
0.1%
```

The important operational idea:

> If the error budget is being consumed too quickly, slow down risky releases and prioritize reliability work.

---

# 15\. Design an end-to-end observability architecture for an ecommerce platform

This is the **capstone question**. I would answer it like this:

```
                    ┌─────────────────┐
                    │   Customers     │
                    └────────┬────────┘
                             │
                       HTTPS / Trace
                             │
                    ┌────────▼────────┐
                    │ API Gateway     │
                    └────────┬────────┘
                             │
             ┌───────────────┼────────────────┐
             │               │                │
             ▼               ▼                ▼
        Order Service   Payment Service   Catalog Service
             │               │
             ▼               ▼
          Kafka          Payment Provider
             │
       ┌─────┴─────┐
       ▼           ▼
 Inventory      Shipping
 Service        Service
       │
       ▼
   PostgreSQL

Applications
     │
     ├── Metrics ──────→ Prometheus ──→ Grafana
     │
     ├── Traces ───────→ OTel Collector ──→ Tempo/Jaeger
     │
     └── Logs ─────────→ OTel Collector ──→ Loki/ELK

                    ↓
             Alerting / On-call
```

### Instrumentation

Every service emits:

```
Metrics
Traces
Logs
```

with common context:

```
traceId
spanId
service.name
environment
version
```

### Metrics

I'd monitor the **four golden signals**:

```
Latency
Traffic
Errors
Saturation
```

Plus platform-specific metrics:

```
JVM / GC
CPU / memory
DB pool
Kafka lag
thread pools
HTTP connection pools
cache hit ratio
```

### Logs

Use structured JSON:

```
{
  "timestamp": "...",
  "level": "ERROR",
  "service": "order-service",
  "traceId": "abc123",
  "spanId": "def456",
  "orderId": "ORD-123",
  "message": "Payment failed"
}
```

Now an engineer can start with:

```
orderId
   ↓
traceId
   ↓
distributed trace
   ↓
failed service/span
   ↓
logs + metrics
   ↓
root cause
```

### Alerting

Don't alert on every individual technical metric.

Create alerts around **customer impact and SLOs**:

```
Checkout success rate ↓
Payment failure rate ↑
p99 checkout latency ↑
Kafka lag ↑
Database saturation ↑
Error-budget burn rate ↑
```

### The interview-winning conclusion

If they ask you to summarize the architecture, say:

> **"I want a unified observability model where metrics tell me that something is wrong, traces tell me where the latency or failure occurred, and logs tell me why. OpenTelemetry provides standardized telemetry and context propagation, while Prometheus/Grafana handle metrics and visualization. For an ecommerce system, I would additionally correlate technical telemetry with business identifiers such as orderId and sagaId, because ultimately we need to understand not only whether the infrastructure is healthy, but whether customers can successfully complete checkout."**

That final distinction—**technical health + business health**—is what separates a basic monitoring answer from a strong senior-level observability answer.

---
Yes. The best way to learn these 15 questions is to build **one small production-style Spring Boot microservices project** where every question appears naturally.

I’d structure it as an **E-commerce Observability Lab**:

```
ecommerce-observability/
│
├── api-gateway/
├── order-service/
├── payment-service/
├── inventory-service/
├── notification-service/
├── kafka/
├── observability/
│   ├── otel-collector/
│   ├── prometheus/
│   ├── grafana/
│   └── loki/
└── docker-compose.yml
```

### What the project will demonstrate

| Question | Code/Scenario |
| --- | --- |
| Slow API | Artificial slow DB/API call |
| Distributed tracing | Gateway → Order → Payment → Inventory |
| Trace ID / Span ID | Structured logs |
| Context propagation | HTTP + Kafka |
| Micrometer | Application metrics |
| Prometheus | Metrics scraping |
| Grafana | Dashboards |
| OpenTelemetry | Traces |
| OTel Collector | Telemetry pipeline |
| High CPU | CPU-intensive endpoint |
| Memory leak | Deliberately retained objects |
| DB pool exhaustion | Slow transactions + small Hikari pool |
| Cascading failure | Dependency slowdown |
| Retry/timeout/circuit breaker | Resilience4j |
| Kafka lag/DLQ | Failed consumer + retry/DLT |
| Saga observability | Order → Payment → Inventory → compensation |
| SLI/SLO/SLA | Prometheus recording/alerting rules |
| E2E architecture | Everything combined |

### Technology stack

I'd use:

- Java 21
- Spring Boot 3.x
- Spring Cloud Gateway
- Spring Web
- Spring Data JPA
- PostgreSQL
- Kafka
- Resilience4j
- Micrometer
- Prometheus
- Grafana
- OpenTelemetry
- OTel Collector
- Loki
- Docker Compose

### One important point

I wouldn't dump 5,000+ lines of code into one response. It would be difficult to understand and debug.

Instead, I can build it **service-by-service**, with each service being complete and runnable. The learning sequence would be:

**Part 1 — Foundation**

1. Docker Compose
2. API Gateway
3. Order Service
4. PostgreSQL
5. Basic REST communication

**Part 2 — Observability**\
6\. Micrometer metrics\
7\. Prometheus\
8\. Grafana\
9\. Structured logging\
10\. OpenTelemetry\
11\. OTel Collector\
12\. Distributed tracing

**Part 3 — Failure Engineering**\
13\. Slow API\
14\. CPU problem\
15\. Memory/GC problem\
16\. DB connection-pool exhaustion\
17\. Timeout/retry/circuit breaker\
18\. Cascading failure

**Part 4 — Kafka**\
19\. Kafka producer\
20\. Kafka consumer\
21\. Consumer lag\
22\. Retry\
23\. Dead-letter topic\
24\. Trace-context propagation through Kafka

**Part 5 — Saga**\
25\. Order Saga\
26\. Payment compensation\
27\. Inventory compensation\
28\. Saga ID \+ Order ID correlation\
29\. Saga observability

**Part 6 — SRE**\
30\. SLI/SLO\
31\. Error budget\
32\. Prometheus alerts\
33\. Grafana dashboards\
34\. End-to-end troubleshooting exercises

For example, the first request would flow like:

```
POST /orders
      │
      ▼
 API Gateway
      │
      │ traceId = 7f82...
      ▼
 Order Service
      │
      ├──────────────► PostgreSQL
      │
      ├── REST ──────► Payment Service
      │                    │
      │                    └── PostgreSQL
      │
      └── Kafka ─────► Inventory Service
                           │
                           └── PostgreSQL

             ┌──────────────────────┐
             │ OpenTelemetry        │
             │       Collector      │
             └──────────┬───────────┘
                        │
              ┌─────────┼─────────┐
              ▼         ▼         ▼
           Traces    Metrics     Logs
              │         │         │
              ▼         ▼         ▼
            Tempo    Prometheus   Loki
                        │
                        ▼
                     Grafana
```

And we'll deliberately introduce failures such as:

```
/payment/slow
/payment/cpu
/payment/memory-leak
/payment/db-exhaustion
```

so you can actually **observe the problem rather than just read about it**.

If you want, I can start with **Part 1 and provide the complete runnable project code (Docker Compose + API Gateway + Order Service + Payment Service + PostgreSQL), including every file and exact commands to run it.**

---
Absolutely. We’ll build it as a **real runnable lab**, not pseudocode. Part 1 will establish the base that later lets us demonstrate tracing, metrics, Kafka, failures, retries, circuit breakers, and Saga behavior.

## Part 1 — Foundation

### Architecture

```
                    ┌──────────────────┐
                    │     Client       │
                    └────────┬─────────┘
                             │
                             │ HTTP :8080
                             ▼
                    ┌──────────────────┐
                    │   API Gateway    │
                    │     :8080        │
                    └────────┬─────────┘
                             │
                             │ HTTP
                             ▼
                    ┌──────────────────┐
                    │  Order Service   │
                    │     :8081        │
                    └───────┬──────────┘
                            │
                  ┌─────────┴──────────┐
                  │                    │
                  ▼                    ▼
          ┌───────────────┐    ┌───────────────┐
          │   PostgreSQL  │    │Payment Service│
          │     :5432     │    │     :8082     │
          └───────────────┘    └───────────────┘
```

We'll initially keep the services simple. Observability comes in the next part.

# 1\. Project structure

Create:

ecommerce-observability/

├── api-gateway/

---

# 2\. Docker Compose

Create `docker-compose.yml`:

```
services:

  postgres:
    image: postgres:16
    container_name: ecommerce-postgres
    environment:
      POSTGRES_DB: ecommerce
      POSTGRES_USER: ecommerce
      POSTGRES_PASSWORD: ecommerce
    ports:
      - "5432:5432"
    volumes:
      - postgres-data:/var/lib/postgresql/data
    healthcheck:
      test: ["CMD-SHELL", "pg_isready -U ecommerce -d ecommerce"]
      interval: 5s
      timeout: 5s
      retries: 10

volumes:
  postgres-data:
```

Start PostgreSQL:

```
docker compose up -d
```

Verify:

```
docker ps
```

You should see:

```
ecommerce-postgres
```

---

# 3\. API Gateway

The gateway will eventually become the entry point for tracing and correlation.

## `api-gateway/pom.xml`

```
<?xml version="1.0" encoding="UTF-8"?>
<project xmlns="http://maven.apache.org/POM/4.0.0"
         xmlns:xsi="http://www.w3.org/2001/XMLSchema-instance"
         xsi:schemaLocation="
           http://maven.apache.org/POM/4.0.0
           https://maven.apache.org/xsd/maven-4.0.0.xsd">

    <modelVersion>4.0.0</modelVersion>

    <parent>
        <groupId>org.springframework.boot</groupId>
        <artifactId>spring-boot-starter-parent</artifactId>
        <version>3.5.6</version>
        <relativePath/>
    </parent>

    <groupId>com.example</groupId>
    <artifactId>api-gateway</artifactId>
    <version>1.0.0</version>

    <properties>
        <java.version>21</java.version>
        <spring-cloud.version>2025.0.0</spring-cloud.version>
    </properties>

    <dependencies>

        <dependency>
            <groupId>org.springframework.cloud</groupId>
            <artifactId>spring-cloud-starter-gateway-server-webflux</artifactId>
        </dependency>

        <dependency>
            <groupId>org.springframework.boot</groupId>
            <artifactId>spring-boot-starter-actuator</artifactId>
        </dependency>

    </dependencies>

    <dependencyManagement>
        <dependencies>
            <dependency>
                <groupId>org.springframework.cloud</groupId>
                <artifactId>spring-cloud-dependencies</artifactId>
                <version>${spring-cloud.version}</version>
                <type>pom</type>
                <scope>import</scope>
            </dependency>
        </dependencies>
    </dependencyManagement>

    <build>
        <plugins>
            <plugin>
                <groupId>org.springframework.boot</groupId>
                <artifactId>spring-boot-maven-plugin</artifactId>
            </plugin>
        </plugins>
    </build>

</project>
```

## `GatewayApplication.java`

```
package com.example.gateway;

import org.springframework.boot.SpringApplication;
import org.springframework.boot.autoconfigure.SpringBootApplication;

@SpringBootApplication
public class GatewayApplication {

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        SpringApplication.run(GatewayApplication.class, args);
    }
}
```

## `application.yml`

```
server:
  port: 8080

spring:
  application:
    name: api-gateway

  cloud:
    gateway:
      routes:

        - id: order-service
          uri: http://localhost:8081
          predicates:
            - Path=/orders/**

        - id: payment-service
          uri: http://localhost:8082
          predicates:
            - Path=/payments/**
```

Run:

```
cd api-gateway
mvn spring-boot:run
```

# 5\. Order Entity

Create:

`entity/Order.java`

```
package com.example.order.entity;

import jakarta.persistence.*;

import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.time.LocalDateTime;

@Entity
@Table(name = "orders")
public class Order {

    @Id
    @GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY)
    private Long id;

    private String customerId;

    private BigDecimal amount;

    private String status;

    private LocalDateTime createdAt;

    public Order() {
    }

    public Order(
            String customerId,
            BigDecimal amount,
            String status,
            LocalDateTime createdAt) {

        this.customerId = customerId;
        this.amount = amount;
        this.status = status;
        this.createdAt = createdAt;
    }

    public Long getId() {
        return id;
    }

    public String getCustomerId() {
        return customerId;
    }

    public BigDecimal getAmount() {
        return amount;
    }

    public String getStatus() {
        return status;
    }

    public LocalDateTime getCreatedAt() {
        return createdAt;
    }

    public void setStatus(String status) {
        this.status = status;
    }
}
```

# 7\. Payment Client

For now we'll use Java's HTTP client through Spring's `RestClient`.

`client/PaymentClient.java`

```
package com.example.order.client;

import org.springframework.stereotype.Component;
import org.springframework.web.client.RestClient;

import java.math.BigDecimal;

@Component
public class PaymentClient {

    private final RestClient restClient;

    public PaymentClient() {
        this.restClient = RestClient.builder()
                .baseUrl("http://localhost:8082")
                .build();
    }

    public PaymentResponse processPayment(
            Long orderId,
            BigDecimal amount) {

        return restClient.post()
                .uri("/payments")
                .body(new PaymentRequest(orderId, amount))
                .retrieve()
                .body(PaymentResponse.class);
    }

    public record PaymentRequest(
            Long orderId,
            BigDecimal amount) {
    }

    public record PaymentResponse(
            Long paymentId,
            Long orderId,
            String status) {
    }
}
```

---

# 8\. Order Service

`service/OrderService.java`

```
package com.example.order.service;

import com.example.order.client.PaymentClient;
import com.example.order.entity.Order;
import com.example.order.repository.OrderRepository;
import org.springframework.stereotype.Service;
import org.springframework.transaction.annotation.Transactional;

import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.time.LocalDateTime;

@Service
public class OrderService {

    private final OrderRepository orderRepository;
    private final PaymentClient paymentClient;

    public OrderService(
            OrderRepository orderRepository,
            PaymentClient paymentClient) {

        this.orderRepository = orderRepository;
        this.paymentClient = paymentClient;
    }

    @Transactional
    public Order createOrder(
            String customerId,
            BigDecimal amount) {

        Order order = new Order(
                customerId,
                amount,
                "PAYMENT_PENDING",
                LocalDateTime.now()
        );

        order = orderRepository.save(order);

        var payment = paymentClient.processPayment(
                order.getId(),
                amount
        );

        if ("SUCCESS".equals(payment.status())) {
            order.setStatus("CONFIRMED");
        } else {
            order.setStatus("PAYMENT_FAILED");
        }

        return orderRepository.save(order);
    }
}
```

---

# 9\. Order Controller

`controller/OrderController.java`

```
package com.example.order.controller;

import com.example.order.entity.Order;
import com.example.order.service.OrderService;
import org.springframework.http.ResponseEntity;
import org.springframework.web.bind.annotation.*;

import java.math.BigDecimal;

@RestController
@RequestMapping("/orders")
public class OrderController {

    private final OrderService orderService;

    public OrderController(OrderService orderService) {
        this.orderService = orderService;
    }

    @PostMapping
    public ResponseEntity<Order> createOrder(
            @RequestParam String customerId,
            @RequestParam BigDecimal amount) {

        Order order = orderService.createOrder(
                customerId,
                amount
        );

        return ResponseEntity.ok(order);
    }

    @GetMapping("/{id}")
    public ResponseEntity<Order> getOrder(
            @PathVariable Long id) {

        return orderService
                .findById(id)
                .map(ResponseEntity::ok)
                .orElse(ResponseEntity.notFound().build());
    }
}
```

We need to add those lookup methods to `OrderService`.

Add:

```
public java.util.Optional<Order> findById(Long id) {
    return orderRepository.findById(id);
}
```

So the complete bottom of `OrderService` becomes:

```
    public java.util.Optional<Order> findById(Long id) {
        return orderRepository.findById(id);
    }
}
```

# 12\. Payment Controller

`controller/PaymentController.java`

```
package com.example.payment.controller;

import org.springframework.web.bind.annotation.*;

import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.util.concurrent.atomic.AtomicLong;

@RestController
@RequestMapping("/payments")
public class PaymentController {

    private final AtomicLong paymentSequence =
            new AtomicLong(1000);

    @PostMapping
    public PaymentResponse processPayment(
            @RequestBody PaymentRequest request) {

        long paymentId =
                paymentSequence.incrementAndGet();

        return new PaymentResponse(
                paymentId,
                request.orderId(),
                "SUCCESS"
        );
    }

    public record PaymentRequest(
            Long orderId,
            BigDecimal amount) {
    }

    public record PaymentResponse(
            Long paymentId,
            Long orderId,
            String status) {
    }
}
```

# 18\. What you have learned already

This small application gives us the foundation for several interview questions.

### Question 1 — Slow API

We can make:

```
Order → Payment
```

slow and investigate where the latency comes from.

### Question 2 — Distributed request

We already have:

```
Gateway
   ↓
Order
   ↓
Payment
```

Later we'll expand this to:

```
Gateway
   ↓
Order
   ├── Payment
   ├── Inventory
   └── Kafka
          ↓
      Notification
```

### Question 3 — Trace ID / Span ID

We'll add tracing so a single request becomes:

```
Trace ID: abc123

Gateway
  Span: 001

Order
  Span: 002

Payment
  Span: 003

PostgreSQL
  Span: 004
```

### Question 5 — Micrometer / Prometheus / Grafana

We'll expose:

```
/order requests
/payment requests
HTTP latency
HTTP errors
JVM memory
JVM CPU
GC
DB pool
```

and visualize them.

### Question 11 — Retry / timeout / circuit breaker

We'll deliberately make Payment fail:

```
Order
  ↓
Payment
  ↓
Timeout
  ↓
Retry
  ↓
Circuit breaker
  ↓
Fallback
```

### Question 13 — Saga

Eventually:

```
Create Order
     ↓
Reserve Inventory
     ↓
Charge Payment
     ↓
Create Shipment
```

and then deliberately fail:

```
Shipment ❌

      ↓

Compensate Payment
      ↓
Release Inventory
      ↓
Order = CANCELLED
```

# Next: Part 2 — Observability

The next layer is where this becomes an **interview-grade observability project**.

We'll add:

```
                    ┌───────────────┐
                    │ API Gateway   │
                    └───────┬───────┘
                            │
                    ┌───────▼───────┐
                    │ Order Service │
                    └───────┬───────┘
                            │
                    ┌───────▼───────┐
                    │Payment Service│
                    └───────────────┘
                            │
              ┌─────────────┼──────────────┐
              ▼             ▼              ▼
           Metrics        Traces          Logs
              │             │              │
              ▼             ▼              ▼
         Prometheus        OTel           Loki
              │          Collector          │
              │             │              │
              └─────────────┼──────────────┘
                            ▼
                         Grafana
```

Then we'll be able to answer the first **6 interview questions hands-on**, including actually following one request using its **Trace ID → Span IDs → logs → metrics → downstream service**.

---
## Part 2 — Metrics, Tracing, Prometheus, Grafana and OpenTelemetry

We now turn the Part 1 application into an actual observability lab.

Spring Boot uses **Micrometer Observation** for metrics/tracing, and its OpenTelemetry support can export traces through OTLP when Micrometer Tracing is configured. OpenTelemetry's Spring Boot starter is another supported instrumentation option.  Home+1

For this lab, I'll use **Spring Boot + Micrometer Tracing + OpenTelemetry OTLP** rather than mixing two tracing approaches. That keeps the architecture easier to understand.

---

# 1\. Target architecture

After this part, your system will look like this:

```
                         ┌─────────────────┐
                         │     Client      │
                         └────────┬────────┘
                                  │
                                  ▼
                         ┌─────────────────┐
                         │  API Gateway    │
                         │     :8080       │
                         └────────┬────────┘
                                  │
                            HTTP + Trace
                                  │
                                  ▼
                         ┌─────────────────┐
                         │  Order Service  │
                         │     :8081       │
                         └───────┬─────────┘
                                 │
                         HTTP + Trace
                                 │
                                 ▼
                         ┌─────────────────┐
                         │ Payment Service │
                         │     :8082       │
                         └─────────────────┘

       ┌──────────────────────────────────────────┐
       │              Observability               │
       │                                          │
       │ Metrics ──► Prometheus ──► Grafana      │
       │                                          │
       │ Traces ──► OTel Collector ──► Jaeger    │
       │                                          │
       │ Logs ──► Console                         │
       └──────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

This gives you the foundation for questions **1–6**.

---

# 2\. Add Prometheus + Grafana + OTel Collector + Jaeger

Replace your existing `docker-compose.yml` with:

```
services:

  postgres:
    image: postgres:16
    container_name: ecommerce-postgres
    environment:
      POSTGRES_DB: ecommerce
      POSTGRES_USER: ecommerce
      POSTGRES_PASSWORD: ecommerce
    ports:
      - "5432:5432"
    volumes:
      - postgres-data:/var/lib/postgresql/data
    healthcheck:
      test: ["CMD-SHELL", "pg_isready -U ecommerce -d ecommerce"]
      interval: 5s
      timeout: 5s
      retries: 10

  prometheus:
    image: prom/prometheus:v3.8.0
    container_name: ecommerce-prometheus
    ports:
      - "9090:9090"
    volumes:
      - ./observability/prometheus/prometheus.yml:/etc/prometheus/prometheus.yml
    depends_on:
      - order-service
      - payment-service

  grafana:
    image: grafana/grafana:12.2.0
    container_name: ecommerce-grafana
    ports:
      - "3000:3000"
    depends_on:
      - prometheus
      - jaeger

  jaeger:
    image: jaegertracing/all-in-one:1.76
    container_name: ecommerce-jaeger
    ports:
      - "16686:16686"
      - "4317:4317"
      - "4318:4318"

  otel-collector:
    image: otel/opentelemetry-collector-contrib:0.138.0
    container_name: ecommerce-otel-collector
    command:
      - "--config=/etc/otelcol-contrib/config.yaml"
    volumes:
      - ./observability/otel/otel-collector.yml:/etc/otelcol-contrib/config.yaml
    ports:
      - "4319:4317"
      - "4320:4318"
      - "8888:8888"
    depends_on:
      - jaeger

volumes:
  postgres-data:
```

Notice something important:

```
Application
     │
     │ OTLP
     ▼
OTel Collector
     │
     ▼
Jaeger
```

That is the pattern we'll use later in production architectures.

---

# 3\. Prometheus configuration

Create:

```
observability/
└── prometheus/
    └── prometheus.yml
```

Put this inside:

```
global:
  scrape_interval: 5s

  evaluation_interval: 5s

scrape_configs:

  - job_name: order-service
    metrics_path: /actuator/prometheus
    static_configs:
      - targets:
          - host.docker.internal:8081

  - job_name: payment-service
    metrics_path: /actuator/prometheus
    static_configs:
      - targets:
          - host.docker.internal:8082

  - job_name: api-gateway
    metrics_path: /actuator/prometheus
    static_configs:
      - targets:
          - host.docker.internal:8080
```

This teaches an important concept:

> **Prometheus pulls metrics from applications.**

It does not normally wait for applications to push metrics to it.

---

# 4\. OpenTelemetry Collector

Create:

```
observability/
└── otel/
    └── otel-collector.yml
```

Use:

```
receivers:

  otlp:
    protocols:
      grpc:
        endpoint: 0.0.0.0:4317

      http:
        endpoint: 0.0.0.0:4318

processors:

  batch:

exporters:

  debug:
    verbosity: basic

  otlp/jaeger:
    endpoint: jaeger:4317
    tls:
      insecure: true

service:

  pipelines:

    traces:
      receivers:
        - otlp

      processors:
        - batch

      exporters:
        - otlp/jaeger
        - debug
```

The flow is:

```
Order Service
      │
      │ OTLP
      ▼
OTel Collector
      │
      ├──► Jaeger
      │
      └──► Debug output
```

The Collector becomes extremely important when we later discuss:

- sampling
- filtering
- batching
- enrichment
- routing
- multiple observability backends

```
<dependency>

</dependency>

<dependency>
    <groupId>io.micrometer</groupId>
    <artifactId>micrometer-registry-prometheus</artifactId>

So your dependencies become:

    </dependency>

Spring Boot recommends Micrometer for metrics, while Micrometer Tracing can bridge to OpenTelemetry for tracing.  Home

# 8\. Configure Order Service

Replace `application.yml` with:

```
server:
  port: 8081

spring:

  application:
    name: order-service

  datasource:
    url: jdbc:postgresql://localhost:5432/ecommerce
    username: ecommerce
    password: ecommerce

  jpa:
    hibernate:
      ddl-auto: update

    show-sql: true

    properties:
      hibernate:
        format_sql: true

management:

  endpoints:
    web:
      exposure:
        include:
          - health
          - info
          - metrics
          - prometheus

  endpoint:
    health:
      show-details: always

  tracing:
    sampling:
      probability: 1.0

  otlp:
    tracing:
      endpoint: http://localhost:4320/v1/traces

logging:
  pattern:
    correlation: "[%X{traceId}/%X{spanId}] "
```

The important settings are:

```
management:
  tracing:
    sampling:
      probability: 1.0
```

For a learning environment, we sample **100%** of traces.

In production, you normally don't blindly sample everything.

# 10\. Gateway configuration

```
server:
  port: 8080

spring:

  application:
    name: api-gateway

  cloud:
    gateway:

      routes:

        - id: order-service
          uri: http://localhost:8081
          predicates:
            - Path=/orders/**

        - id: payment-service
          uri: http://localhost:8082
          predicates:
            - Path=/payments/**

management:

  endpoints:
    web:
      exposure:
        include:
          - health
          - info
          - metrics
          - prometheus

  tracing:
    sampling:
      probability: 1.0

  otlp:
    tracing:
      endpoint: http://localhost:4320/v1/traces

logging:
  pattern:
    correlation: "[%X{traceId}/%X{spanId}] "
```

---

# 11\. Important correction to our Part 1 code

There's one architectural improvement we should make now.

Our `PaymentClient` currently creates a `RestClient` manually:

```
RestClient.builder()
    .baseUrl("http://localhost:8082")
    .build();
```

For a tracing-aware application, let Spring manage the HTTP client.

Replace `PaymentClient.java` with:

```
package com.example.order.client;

import org.springframework.stereotype.Component;
import org.springframework.web.client.RestClient;

import java.math.BigDecimal;

@Component
public class PaymentClient {

    private final RestClient restClient;

    public PaymentClient(RestClient.Builder builder) {

        this.restClient = builder
                .baseUrl("http://localhost:8082")
                .build();
    }

    public PaymentResponse processPayment(
            Long orderId,
            BigDecimal amount) {

        return restClient
                .post()
                .uri("/payments")
                .body(new PaymentRequest(orderId, amount))
                .retrieve()
                .body(PaymentResponse.class);
    }

    public record PaymentRequest(
            Long orderId,
            BigDecimal amount) {
    }

    public record PaymentResponse(
            Long paymentId,
            Long orderId,
            String status) {
    }
}
```

This is important for understanding automatic instrumentation/context propagation.

---

# 12\. Add custom business metrics

Automatic HTTP metrics are useful, but interviewers often ask:

> "How would you expose a business metric?"

Let's add one.

Modify `OrderService.java`:

```
package com.example.order.service;

import com.example.order.client.PaymentClient;
import com.example.order.entity.Order;
import com.example.order.repository.OrderRepository;
import io.micrometer.core.instrument.Counter;
import io.micrometer.core.instrument.MeterRegistry;
import org.springframework.stereotype.Service;
import org.springframework.transaction.annotation.Transactional;

import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.time.LocalDateTime;
import java.util.Optional;

@Service
public class OrderService {

    private final OrderRepository orderRepository;

    private final PaymentClient paymentClient;

    private final Counter ordersCreated;

    public OrderService(
            OrderRepository orderRepository,
            PaymentClient paymentClient,
            MeterRegistry meterRegistry) {

        this.orderRepository = orderRepository;
        this.paymentClient = paymentClient;

        this.ordersCreated = Counter.builder("orders.created")
                .description("Number of orders created")
                .register(meterRegistry);
    }

    @Transactional
    public Order createOrder(
            String customerId,
            BigDecimal amount) {

        Order order = new Order(
                customerId,
                amount,
                "PAYMENT_PENDING",
                LocalDateTime.now()
        );

        order = orderRepository.save(order);

        ordersCreated.increment();

        var payment = paymentClient.processPayment(
                order.getId(),
                amount
        );

        if ("SUCCESS".equals(payment.status())) {
            order.setStatus("CONFIRMED");
        } else {
            order.setStatus("PAYMENT_FAILED");
        }

        return orderRepository.save(order);
    }

    public Optional<Order> findById(Long id) {
        return orderRepository.findById(id);
    }
}
```

Now we've created:

```
orders.created
```

This is a **business metric**, rather than merely a technical metric.

---

# 13\. Add payment business metrics

Replace `PaymentController` with:

```
package com.example.payment.controller;

import io.micrometer.core.instrument.Counter;
import io.micrometer.core.instrument.MeterRegistry;
import org.springframework.web.bind.annotation.*;

import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.util.concurrent.atomic.AtomicLong;

@RestController
@RequestMapping("/payments")
public class PaymentController {

    private final AtomicLong paymentSequence =
            new AtomicLong(1000);

    private final Counter paymentsProcessed;

    public PaymentController(
            MeterRegistry meterRegistry) {

        this.paymentsProcessed =
                Counter.builder("payments.processed")
                        .description("Number of payments processed")
                        .register(meterRegistry);
    }

    @PostMapping
    public PaymentResponse processPayment(
            @RequestBody PaymentRequest request) {

        long paymentId =
                paymentSequence.incrementAndGet();

        paymentsProcessed.increment();

        return new PaymentResponse(
                paymentId,
                request.orderId(),
                "SUCCESS"
        );
    }

    public record PaymentRequest(
            Long orderId,
            BigDecimal amount) {
    }

    public record PaymentResponse(
            Long paymentId,
            Long orderId,
            String status) {
    }
}
```

Now:

```
payments.processed
```

will be available.

order-service
│
├── HTTP POST /orders
│
└── payment-service
      │

```

This is the answer to:

> **How do you trace a request across 10 microservices?**

You don't manually grep every server.

You use the distributed trace.

---

# 24\. What just happened?

This one request:

```
POST /orders
```

created a chain:

```
Client
   │
   ▼
Gateway
   │
   │ Trace Context
   ▼
Order Service
   │
   │ Trace Context
   ▼
Payment Service
```

The tracing context travels between services.

Conceptually:

```
Trace ID
    │
    ├── Gateway Span
    │
    ├── Order Span
    │
    └── Payment Span
```

That's the key difference:

### Trace ID

Represents the entire request.

### Span ID

Represents an individual operation.

---

# 25\. Why this is powerful

Suppose this happens:

```
POST /orders
Total latency = 4.2 seconds
```

Jaeger might show:

```
Gateway              4.2 sec
└── Order             4.1 sec
    ├── PostgreSQL    50 ms
    └── Payment       3.9 sec
```

Immediately we know:

```
Database = probably fine

Payment Service = suspect
```

Then you drill into Payment.

This is exactly how you approach the **"slow API in production"** interview question.

@GetMapping("/slow")

}

Spring Boot describes observability as the three pillars of **logging, metrics and traces**, with Micrometer used for metrics/tracing integration.  Home

---

## One architectural distinction to remember

Don't say:

> "Prometheus is an observability tool that creates metrics."

Instead:

> **"Micrometer instruments the application and provides metrics through Actuator; Prometheus scrapes and stores those metrics; Grafana queries Prometheus and visualizes them."**

And don't say:

> "OpenTelemetry is Grafana."

Instead:

> **"OpenTelemetry provides vendor-neutral instrumentation and telemetry pipelines; the OTel Collector receives, processes and exports telemetry to backends such as Jaeger."**

OpenTelemetry supports traces, metrics and logs, while Spring Boot's current guidance specifically recommends Micrometer for application metrics and supports OpenTelemetry for tracing.  OpenTelemetry+1
