# 17\. The production troubleshooting exercise

This is where the project becomes useful for your interview.

## Scenario 1 — API suddenly becomes slow

You see:

```
p95 latency
200ms → 4 seconds
```

Investigate:

```
Grafana
   ↓
HTTP latency
   ↓
Trace
   ↓
Payment span = 3.5 sec
   ↓
Payment logs
   ↓
External provider timeout
```

Conclusion:

```
Root cause:
Payment provider latency
```

---

## Scenario 2 — CPU reaches 95%

```
Prometheus
   ↓
process_cpu_usage
   ↓
payment-service
   ↓
thread dump / profiling
   ↓
hot method
   ↓
infinite calculation / excessive loop
```

---

## Scenario 3 — Memory keeps increasing

```
Heap ↑
   ↓
GC frequency ↑
   ↓
GC pause ↑
   ↓
Heap dump
   ↓
retained objects
   ↓
memory leak
```

---

## Scenario 4 — Database pool exhausted

```
Hikari active = 50
Hikari max = 50
Hikari pending = 100
```

Then:

```
API latency ↑
   ↓
requests waiting for DB connections
```

---

## Scenario 5 — Kafka lag increases

```
Producer rate:
500 msg/sec

Consumer rate:
100 msg/sec
```

Therefore:

```
lag grows by ~400 msg/sec
```

Investigate:

```
consumer CPU
consumer processing latency
DB dependency
downstream APIs
consumer errors
rebalances
partition distribution
```

---

## Scenario 6 — DLQ starts growing

```
order.created.DLT
       │
       ▼
DLQ messages ↑
       │
       ▼
Inspect failure reason
       │
       ▼
Poison message?
       │
       ├── yes → fix data / replay
       │
       └── no → investigate consumer
```

---

# 18\. Your final interview mental model

When the interviewer gives you **any production problem**, start here:

```
                INCIDENT
                   │
                   ▼
               METRICS
                   │
                   ▼
          Which service?
                   │
                   ▼
                TRACE
                   │
                   ▼
          Which dependency?
                   │
                   ▼
                 LOGS
                   │
                   ▼
             ROOT CAUSE
                   │
                   ▼
              MITIGATION
                   │
                   ▼
             PERMANENT FIX
                   │
                   ▼
            SLO / ERROR BUDGET
```

And when designing a new microservice:

```
              MICROSERVICE
                   │
       ┌───────────┼───────────┐
       ▼           ▼           ▼
    Metrics       Logs       Traces
       │           │           │
       └───────────┼───────────┘
                   ▼
              Monitoring
                   │
                   ▼
                Alerts
                   │
                   ▼
              SLO / SLI
```

For asynchronous workflows:

```
Service
   ↓
Transactional Outbox
   ↓
Kafka
   ↓
Consumer
   ↓
Retry
   ↓
DLQ
   ↓
Compensation
```

This is the architecture you should be able to **draw and explain without code** in an interview.

# Part 7 — Production Failure Lab

Now let's move from **building the application** to **debugging it like a production engineer**.

We'll deliberately create failures and learn the exact investigation sequence. This directly prepares you for questions **1, 7, 8, 9, 10, and 11**.

## 1\. Slow API in production

Suppose the interviewer says:

> "The order API was normally 200 ms, but suddenly p95 is 5 seconds. How do you debug it?"

Don't immediately say "check logs."

Use this sequence:

```
Alert
  ↓
Metrics
  ↓
Identify affected endpoint/service
  ↓
Distributed trace
  ↓
Find slow span
  ↓
Check dependency
  ↓
Logs
  ↓
Confirm root cause
  ↓
Mitigate
```

### Step 1 — Check request rate

Prometheus:

```
rate(http_server_requests_seconds_count[5m])
```

Ask:

- Did traffic suddenly increase?
- Is the problem affecting every endpoint?
- Is it only `/orders`?

---

### Step 2 — Check latency

```
histogram_quantile(
  0.95,
  sum by (le) (
    rate(http_server_requests_seconds_bucket[5m])
  )
)
```

If:

```
p50 = 200 ms
p95 = 5 sec
p99 = 10 sec
```

you have a tail-latency problem.

That immediately suggests looking for:

- slow dependencies
- connection-pool waits
- retries
- GC pauses
- lock contention
- network problems

---

# 2\. Use the trace

Suppose Jaeger shows:

```
POST /orders                         5.1s
│
├── PostgreSQL                       50ms
│
├── Kafka                            10ms
│
├── Inventory                        40ms
│
├── Payment                         4.8s
│    └── External payment API       4.7s
│
└── Shipping                         50ms
```

Now you know:

```
Order Service isn't necessarily slow.
Payment dependency is slow.
```

This distinction is important.

---

# 3\. Retry amplification

Now suppose Payment has:

```
timeout = 2 seconds
retries = 3
```

A request could potentially take roughly:

```
2 sec
+
2 sec
+
2 sec
=
6 sec
```

before considering additional processing.

And if multiple services independently retry:

```
Order
  ↓ retry
Payment
  ↓ retry
Provider
```

you can create enormous traffic amplification.

This is why retries are dangerous.

---

# 4\. Retry + timeout \+ circuit breaker

The recommended relationship is generally:

```
Request
  │
  ▼
Timeout
  │
  ▼
Limited Retry
  │
  ▼
Circuit Breaker
  │
  ▼
Fallback
```

But exact ordering depends on the resilience strategy and where each policy is applied.

The important principle is:

> **Retries must be bounded, timeouts must exist, and circuit breakers should prevent repeated calls to an unhealthy dependency.**

---

# 5\. Add Resilience4j

In Payment client:

```
<dependency>
    <groupId>io.github.resilience4j</groupId>
    <artifactId>resilience4j-spring-boot3</artifactId>
</dependency>
```

Then:

```
@Retry(
    name = "paymentProvider"
)
@CircuitBreaker(
    name = "paymentProvider",
    fallbackMethod = "paymentFallback"
)
public PaymentResponse charge(
        PaymentRequest request) {

    return paymentClient.charge(request);
}
```

Timeout configuration:

```
resilience4j:

  retry:

    instances:

      paymentProvider:

        max-attempts: 3

        wait-duration: 500ms

  circuitbreaker:

    instances:

      paymentProvider:

        sliding-window-size: 20

        failure-rate-threshold: 50

        wait-duration-in-open-state: 10s
```

---

# 6\. What happens when Payment goes down?

Without resilience:

```
1000 orders/sec
       │
       ▼
Payment unavailable
       │
       ▼
1000 failing calls/sec
       │
       ▼
Retries
       │
       ▼
3000 calls/sec
       │
       ▼
More failures
```

This can become a cascading failure.

With a circuit breaker:

```
Payment unavailable
       │
       ▼
Failures increase
       │
       ▼
Circuit OPEN
       │
       ▼
Requests fail fast
```

Instead of waiting seconds for every request.

---

# 7\. Circuit breaker states

There are usually three important states:

```
CLOSED
   │
   │ failures increase
   ▼
OPEN
   │
   │ after wait period
   ▼
HALF_OPEN
   │
   ├── success → CLOSED
   │
   └── failure → OPEN
```

### CLOSED

Normal operation.

```
Request → Payment
```

### OPEN

Don't call Payment.

```
Request
   │
   X
Payment
```

Fail fast or use fallback.

### HALF\_OPEN

Allow limited test traffic.

```
Test request
    │
    ▼
Payment healthy?
```

---

# 8\. How to detect cascading failures

Suppose:

```
Payment latency ↑
       ↓
Order latency ↑
       ↓
Threads blocked
       ↓
Request queue ↑
       ↓
CPU ↑
       ↓
More requests timeout
       ↓
Retries ↑
       ↓
Payment receives more traffic
```

This is a feedback loop.

Visualize it as:

```
          ┌─────────────────────┐
          │                     ▼
Payment slow → Order slow → retries
     ▲                         │
     │                         ▼
     └────── more traffic ←────┘
```

That's a cascading failure.

---

# 9\. Metrics to detect it

Watch these together:

```
HTTP request rate
HTTP error rate
HTTP latency
Thread pool usage
CPU
Memory
DB connection pool
Kafka lag
Retry count
Circuit breaker state
Downstream latency
```

The combination is much more valuable than one metric.

---

# 10\. High CPU investigation

Now deliberately create CPU pressure.

```
@GetMapping("/debug/high-cpu")
public String highCpu() {

    while (true) {
        Math.sqrt(
            ThreadLocalRandom.current()
                .nextDouble()
        );
    }
}
```

Don't put this into a production system. It's purely for your local lab.

Call:

```
curl http://localhost:8082/debug/high-cpu
```

CPU should rise.

---

# 11\. First question: application or infrastructure?

If CPU is 95%, ask:

```
Is it:
    JVM?
    Container?
    Host?
```

Then:

```
Which process?
Which thread?
Which method?
```

---

# 12\. JVM investigation

Find the process:

```
jps -lv
```

Then:

```
jstack <PID>
```

For deeper investigation:

```
jcmd <PID> Thread.print
```

Look for:

```
RUNNABLE
```

threads consuming CPU.

---

# 13\. Thread → stack trace

Suppose Linux shows:

```
PID 1234
TID 5678
CPU 98%
```

Convert the thread ID to hexadecimal if necessary and locate the corresponding JVM thread.

Then inspect the stack:

```
Thread-42
RUNNABLE

at PaymentService.highCpu()
at PaymentController.debug()
```

Now you have:

```
CPU
 ↓
Process
 ↓
Thread
 ↓
Stack trace
 ↓
Code
```

That's the investigation chain to remember.

---

# 14\. Memory leak investigation

Create another lab endpoint:

```
private final List<byte[]> leak =
        new ArrayList<>();

@GetMapping("/debug/memory-leak")
public String memoryLeak() {

    while (true) {

        leak.add(
            new byte[1024 * 1024]
        );
    }
}
```

Again, **only for the local lab**.

Now monitor:

```
Heap usage
Old generation
GC frequency
GC pause
```

---

# 15\. What a memory leak looks like

Healthy application:

```
Heap
  │
  │     /\    /\    /\
  │    /  \  /  \  /  \
  │___/____\/____\/____\____
  └──────────────────────────
       GC   GC   GC
```

Memory leak:

```
Heap
  │
  │      /\
  │     /  \
  │    /    \__
  │   /        \__
  │__/            \__
  └────────────────────
```

The important clue:

> **After GC, the baseline keeps increasing.**

---

# 16\. Heap dump

Use:

```
jcmd <PID> GC.heap_dump /tmp/heap.hprof
```

Then inspect the heap dump with a profiler such as Eclipse MAT or another JVM analysis tool.

Look for:

```
Retained size
Dominators
Large collections
Unexpected caches
Static references
ThreadLocal objects
```

---

# 17\. GC problem vs memory leak

They're related but not identical.

### High GC

Could be:

```
Too many temporary objects
High allocation rate
Small heap
Large traffic spike
```

### Memory leak

Typically:

```
Objects remain reachable
Old-generation usage keeps increasing
GC cannot reclaim them
Eventually OutOfMemoryError
```

Interview answer:

> "I would first distinguish high allocation/GC pressure from a true leak by observing post-GC heap occupancy. If the post-GC baseline keeps increasing, I'd capture a heap dump and inspect dominators and retained objects."

That's a strong answer.

---

# 18\. Database connection-pool exhaustion

Spring Boot commonly uses HikariCP.

Expose metrics:

```
management:
  endpoints:
    web:
      exposure:
        include:
          - health
          - metrics
          - prometheus
```

Useful metrics include:

```
hikaricp_connections
hikaricp_connections_active
hikaricp_connections_idle
hikaricp_connections_pending
hikaricp_connections_max
```

---

# 19\. Simulate slow database calls

```
@Transactional
public void slowOperation() {

    jdbcTemplate.execute(
        "SELECT pg_sleep(30)"
    );
}
```

Send many concurrent requests.

For example:

```
for i in {1..100}; do
  curl http://localhost:8080/debug/slow-db &
done
wait
```

Now watch Hikari.

You may see:

```
active:   10
idle:      0
pending:  90
max:      10
```

The application isn't necessarily CPU-bound.

It's waiting for connections.

---

# 20\. Connection pool diagnosis

The flow is:

```
HTTP request
     │
     ▼
Need DB connection
     │
     ▼
Pool
     │
     ├── connection available → execute
     │
     └── no connection
             │
             ▼
          WAITING
             │
             ▼
          TIMEOUT
```

This is why increasing the pool size isn't automatically the answer.

If the database itself is overloaded:

```
Pool 10 → DB overloaded
Pool 100 → DB even more overloaded
```

You can make the situation worse.

---

# 21\. Questions to ask for DB pool exhaustion

When you see:

```
active = max
pending ↑
```

investigate:

- Are queries slow?
- Is there a database lock?
- Are connections being leaked?
- Did traffic increase?
- Did a downstream dependency get slow while holding transactions?
- Is the database CPU saturated?
- Are transaction boundaries too large?

---

# 22\. One extremely important production pattern

Never do this:

```
@Transactional
public void process() {

    updateDatabase();

    callExternalPaymentAPI();

    updateDatabase();
}
```

You may hold a DB connection while waiting for an external API.

If Payment takes:

```
10 seconds
```

you may hold the DB connection for those 10 seconds.

At high traffic:

```
DB pool
████████████████████
ALL CONNECTIONS BUSY
```

Better architecture:

```
Short DB transaction
      ↓
Commit
      ↓
External call
      ↓
Short DB transaction
```

Or use asynchronous Saga processing where appropriate.

---

# 23\. The complete production-debugging matrix

| Symptom | First place to look |
| --- | --- |
| API latency ↑ | Metrics → traces |
| Error rate ↑ | Logs + traces |
| CPU ↑ | Process → thread dump |
| Heap ↑ | GC → heap dump |
| DB pool exhausted | Hikari + DB |
| Kafka lag ↑ | Consumer metrics |
| DLQ ↑ | Consumer errors |
| Retry count ↑ | Resilience metrics |
| Circuit OPEN | Downstream health |
| Saga failures ↑ | Saga metrics + traces |

---

# 24\. Your interview answer framework

For almost every production question, answer in this format:

### 1\. Detect

> "I'd first confirm the symptom using metrics."

### 2\. Scope

> "I'd determine whether it's one endpoint, service, instance, region, or dependency."

### 3\. Trace

> "I'd inspect a representative distributed trace to identify where latency or failure occurs."

### 4\. Correlate

> "I'd correlate the trace with structured logs and service metrics."

### 5\. Diagnose

> "Then I'd determine whether the bottleneck is CPU, memory, database, network, Kafka, or a downstream service."

### 6\. Mitigate

> "I'd apply the safest mitigation first—rollback, traffic reduction, circuit breaking, scaling, or disabling the problematic feature."

### 7\. Prevent

> "Finally I'd fix the root cause and add an alert, SLO, test, or instrumentation to prevent recurrence."

This framework is useful for **all 15 questions**, not just one.

---

## Next

The next part should be the **actual interview simulation**: I'll act as the interviewer and give you production scenarios one at a time. You'll answer, and I'll grade each answer like a **Senior/Lead Java Microservices + Observability interview**.

---
## Part 8 — Mock Interview: Question 1

I'll act as the interviewer. **Don't look for the model answer yet**—answer as you would in the real interview. I'll then score your response and improve it.

### Interviewer

> **Your ecommerce platform has 20 Spring Boot microservices.**
>
>  Suddenly, customers report that **`POST /orders` has become very slow**.
>
>  Normally:
>
>  - p50 = 150 ms
> - p95 = 400 ms
    >
    >  Now:
    >
    >  - p50 = 800 ms
> - p95 = 6 seconds
> - p99 = 12 seconds
    >
    >  CPU and memory on the Order Service look normal.
    >
    >  **How would you investigate this production incident?**
    >
    >  Explain your approach step by step, including the observability tools/metrics you would check.

## Model answer

> “First, I would confirm the problem using metrics rather than immediately checking individual logs.
>
>  **1\. Check the scope of the incident.**\
>  I would look at request rate, error rate, p50/p95/p99 latency, and determine whether the latency increase affects all Order Service endpoints or specifically `POST /orders`. I would also check whether it affects all instances or only particular pods/nodes.
>
>  **2\. Check recent changes.**\
>  I would correlate the start of the latency increase with deployments, configuration changes, database changes, infrastructure changes, or changes in downstream services.
>
>  **3\. Use distributed tracing.**\
>  Since CPU and memory are normal, I would take a representative slow request and inspect its trace. I would look at the latency of each span—database calls, Kafka operations, Inventory, Payment, Shipping, and external APIs.
>
>  **4\. Identify the slow dependency.**\
>  For example, if the trace shows:
>
>  `Order Service = 6 seconds`\
>  `PostgreSQL = 50 ms`\
>  `Inventory = 100 ms`\
>  `Payment = 5.5 seconds`
>
>  then I would focus on Payment rather than troubleshooting the Order Service itself.
>
>  **5\. Correlate with logs.**\
>  Using the Trace ID, Order ID, and possibly Saga ID, I would search structured logs across Order and Payment services. I'd look for timeout messages, retries, connection-pool waits, exceptions, or downstream provider failures.
>
>  **6\. Check dependency-specific metrics.**\
>  If it's the database, I'd check query latency, locks, connection-pool active/pending connections, and database CPU. If it's Kafka, I'd check consumer lag, processing latency, and rebalances. If it's an external API, I'd check its latency, timeout and error rate.
>
>  **7\. Check resilience behavior.**\
>  I'd look at retry counts, timeout configuration, and circuit-breaker state. A downstream timeout combined with retries can turn a 2-second dependency problem into a 6–12-second API latency problem.
>
>  **8\. Mitigate first.**\
>  Depending on the root cause, I might roll back a recent deployment, reduce traffic, enable a circuit breaker, temporarily disable a noncritical dependency, or route traffic to a healthy instance/provider.
>
>  **9\. Verify recovery.**\
>  I'd confirm that p95/p99 latency, errors, downstream metrics, and the relevant SLO have returned to normal.
>
>  **10\. Prevent recurrence.**\
>  Finally, I'd identify the root cause and improve instrumentation, timeout/retry policies, alerts, capacity, or code as appropriate.
>
>  So my primary investigation path would be: **metrics → scope → distributed trace → slow dependency → correlated logs → dependency metrics → mitigation → root-cause fix.**”

### Score: 9/10

That's the answer I'd aim for in a **Senior Java Microservices / Observability** interview.

The key phrase to remember is:

> **“Don't assume the service reporting the latency is the service causing the latency.”**

For example:

```
POST /orders = 6 sec
       │
       ▼
Order Service
       │
       ├── DB       50 ms
       ├── Kafka    10 ms
       ├── Inventory 80 ms
       └── Payment  5.6 sec  ← investigate here
```

### Interviewer follow-up

Now I'd challenge you:

> **You inspect the trace and discover that Payment Service is taking 5 seconds because its external payment provider is timing out. Payment Service retries the provider 3 times.**
>
>  **What would you change in the architecture/configuration to prevent this from causing a cascading failure across the ecommerce platform?**

---
Absolutely. For a **Senior Java/Spring Boot Microservices interview**, you should be able to look at a production symptom and quickly connect it to **scaling, latency, throughput, memory, concurrency, traffic, data volume, and architecture**.

The key is not to say _“just increase the pods.”_ You need to explain **why the bottleneck exists, how you prove it, what you do immediately, and what architectural change prevents recurrence.**

# 1\. The master production-debugging model

When an interviewer gives you any backend scenario, start with:

```
                    PRODUCTION PROBLEM
                           │
           ┌───────────────┼────────────────┐
           ▼               ▼                ▼
        Traffic          Latency         Errors
           │               │                │
           └───────────────┼────────────────┘
                           ▼
                     Find bottleneck
                           │
        ┌──────────────────┼──────────────────┐
        ▼                  ▼                  ▼
       CPU              Memory                I/O
        │                  │                  │
        ▼                  ▼                  ▼
   Scale compute       GC/Heap          DB/Kafka/API
                           │
                           ▼
                      Concurrency
                           │
                           ▼
                    Architecture fix
```

Then investigate:

```
Traffic
  ↓
Throughput
  ↓
Latency
  ↓
Concurrency
  ↓
CPU / Memory / I/O
  ↓
Database / Kafka / External APIs
  ↓
Scaling strategy
```

---

# 2\. Horizontal vs vertical scaling

This is one of the most common interview questions.

## Vertical scaling

Make one machine/container bigger.

```
Before:

Service
  │
  └── 4 CPU
      8 GB RAM

After:

Service
  │
  └── 16 CPU
      32 GB RAM
```

Advantages:

- Simple
- No application architecture changes
- Useful for stateful workloads
- Useful when one instance needs more resources

Disadvantages:

- Hardware limit
- Larger blast radius
- Doesn't inherently provide high availability
- Eventually becomes expensive
- Doesn't solve all bottlenecks

---

## Horizontal scaling

Add more instances.

```
             Load Balancer
                  │
        ┌─────────┼─────────┐
        ▼         ▼         ▼
      Pod-1     Pod-2     Pod-3
        │         │         │
        └─────────┼─────────┘
                  ▼
               Database
```

Advantages:

- Better availability
- Handles traffic growth
- Cloud/Kubernetes friendly
- Allows rolling deployments
- Failure of one instance doesn't necessarily take down service

But:

> **Horizontal scaling only helps if the bottleneck is horizontally scalable.**

If every pod waits for the same database:

```
Pod 1 ──┐
Pod 2 ──┤
Pod 3 ──┼──► Database
Pod 4 ──┤
Pod 5 ──┘
```

adding 20 more pods may actually make the database worse.

---

# 3\. Interview scenario: huge traffic

### Interviewer

> Traffic suddenly increases from 1,000 requests/sec to 20,000 requests/sec. What do you do?

A strong answer:

> "First I would determine whether the bottleneck is compute, database, messaging, network, or an external dependency. I would check request rate, CPU, memory, thread-pool utilization, connection pools, latency, error rate, Kafka throughput and database capacity. If the application instances are CPU-bound and stateless, I would horizontally scale behind a load balancer. But I would also verify downstream capacity because scaling the frontend service can amplify traffic to the database and dependencies."

Architecture:

```
                20K RPS
                   │
                   ▼
              Load Balancer
                   │
        ┌──────────┼──────────┐
        ▼          ▼          ▼
       Pod        Pod        Pod
       1          2          3
        │          │          │
        └──────────┼──────────┘
                   ▼
              Cache / DB
```

But don't stop there.

You need to ask:

```
Can DB handle 20K requests/sec?
Can Kafka handle it?
Can downstream APIs handle it?
Can connection pools handle it?
Can network bandwidth handle it?
```

---

# 4\. Latency vs throughput

This distinction is extremely important.

### Latency

How long one request takes.

```
Request
  │
  └──────────────► Response

       200 ms
```

### Throughput

How much work you process per unit of time.

```
1000 requests/sec
```

You can have:

```
High throughput + high latency
```

For example:

```
10,000 requests/sec
p99 = 10 seconds
```

The system processes huge volume but responds slowly.

Or:

```
100 requests/sec
p99 = 50 ms
```

Excellent latency but low throughput.

---

# 5\. Interview scenario: latency increased

### Interviewer

> API latency went from 200 ms to 5 seconds. CPU is only 30%. What could be wrong?

Don't say:

> "Scale horizontally."

Instead:

> "Low CPU suggests the application may be waiting on I/O rather than being compute-bound. I'd inspect distributed traces and look at database latency, connection-pool waits, downstream HTTP calls, Kafka operations, locks, thread-pool saturation and external APIs."

Possible architecture:

```
API
 │
 ├── DB ──────────────── 4.5 sec
 │
 ├── Payment API ─────── 100 ms
 │
 └── Kafka ───────────── 20 ms
```

Scaling API pods won't fix:

```
DB = 4.5 seconds
```

---

# 6\. Concurrency problem

Suppose you have:

```
1000 concurrent requests
```

but only:

```
20 DB connections
```

Then:

```
1000 requests
      │
      ▼
20 DB connections
      │
      ▼
980 waiting
```

Latency increases.

Metrics may show:

```
CPU = 30%
Memory = 40%

DB active connections = 20/20
DB pending connections = 980
```

This is a **concurrency bottleneck**, not necessarily a CPU bottleneck.

---

# 7\. Thread pool exhaustion

Spring Boot/Tomcat might have a limited number of request threads.

Suppose:

```
Tomcat threads = 200
```

And each request waits 10 seconds on an external API.

Then:

```
200 threads
████████████████████
ALL BLOCKED
```

New requests wait.

Eventually:

```
Latency ↑
Timeouts ↑
Errors ↑
```

This can cause a cascading failure.

---

# 8\. Architecture solution for blocking dependencies

Bad:

```
HTTP Request
    │
    ▼
Thread
    │
    ▼
Slow external API
    │
    │ 10 seconds
    ▼
Response
```

Better:

```
Request
  │
  ▼
Queue / Kafka
  │
  ▼
Async Worker
  │
  ▼
External API
  │
  ▼
Event
  │
  ▼
Update Order
```

This removes the long external operation from the synchronous request path when the business process permits asynchronous behavior.

---

# 9\. Memory issue scenario

### Interviewer

> Your service memory keeps increasing from 2 GB to 8 GB and eventually gets killed. How do you investigate?

Answer:

> "I'd first distinguish between normal heap growth, high allocation rate, GC pressure and an actual memory leak. I'd monitor JVM heap, old-generation occupancy, GC frequency and pause time. If the post-GC heap baseline keeps increasing, I'd capture a heap dump and analyze retained objects, dominators, collections, caches and static references."

Architecture:

```
Requests
   │
   ▼
Objects created
   │
   ▼
Heap
   │
   ▼
GC
   │
   ├── reclaimed → healthy
   │
   └── retained → possible leak
```

---

# 10\. Common Java memory problems

In Spring Boot applications, investigate:

```
Huge collections
Unbounded caches
Static collections
ThreadLocal misuse
Large request/response objects
Large Kafka messages
Reading entire files into memory
Unbounded queues
Too many concurrent requests
Excessive object allocation
```

---

# 11\. Huge amount of data

### Interviewer

> Your database contains 500 million orders. The order search API has become slow. What do you do?

Don't immediately increase CPU.

Think:

```
500M rows
    │
    ▼
Query
    │
    ├── Missing index?
    ├── Full table scan?
    ├── Bad join?
    ├── Too much data returned?
    ├── Sorting huge dataset?
    └── Offset pagination?
```

---

# 12\. Offset pagination problem

Suppose:

```
SELECT *
FROM orders
ORDER BY created_at DESC
LIMIT 100
OFFSET 10000000;
```

The database may need to process a huge amount of rows before returning the page.

For very large datasets, consider **keyset/cursor pagination**.

Example:

```
SELECT *
FROM orders
WHERE created_at < :lastSeen
ORDER BY created_at DESC
LIMIT 100;
```

Architecture:

```
Client
  │
  ▼
API
  │
  ▼
Indexed query
  │
  ▼
Only required rows
```

---

# 13\. Huge data + analytics

Don't use the transactional database for everything.

Bad:

```
Orders
  │
  ├── transactions
  ├── dashboards
  ├── analytics
  ├── reports
  └── machine learning
```

Better:

```
                 Order Service
                       │
                       ▼
                     Kafka
                       │
             ┌─────────┼─────────┐
             ▼         ▼         ▼
          OLTP DB   Analytics   Search
                     Store      Index
```

The transactional database handles transactions.

Analytical workloads go elsewhere.

---

# 14\. Read-heavy system

Suppose:

```
Writes = 1,000/sec
Reads  = 100,000/sec
```

Don't necessarily scale the write database vertically.

Use:

```
             Application
                  │
          ┌───────┴────────┐
          ▼                ▼
       Cache            Read DB
          │                │
          │          ┌─────┴─────┐
          │          ▼           ▼
          │       Replica 1   Replica 2
          │
          ▼
       Response
```

Possible technologies:

- Redis/cache
- Read replicas
- Search indexes
- CDN for suitable data

---

# 15\. Write-heavy system

Suppose:

```
100,000 events/sec
```

and the database cannot keep up.

Think:

```
Application
     │
     ▼
Kafka
     │
     ├── partition 1
     ├── partition 2
     ├── partition 3
     ├── ...
     └── partition N
          │
          ▼
      Consumers
          │
          ▼
       Database
```

Kafka provides buffering and allows consumers to process data independently.

But Kafka isn't magic either.

You need to consider:

```
partition count
producer throughput
consumer count
consumer processing time
broker capacity
disk
network
replication
```

---

# 16\. Kafka throughput scenario

### Interviewer

> Producers generate 100,000 messages/sec, but consumers process only 40,000/sec. What happens?

Lag grows:

```
Producer
100K/sec
    │
    ▼
 Kafka
    │
    ▼
Consumer
40K/sec
```

Difference:

```
100K - 40K
= 60K messages/sec
```

backlog growth.

Architecture response:

```
Increase partitions
        ↓
Increase consumer instances
        ↓
Optimize consumer processing
        ↓
Batch DB writes
        ↓
Remove slow downstream dependency
```

But remember:

> A consumer group cannot have more active consumers than useful partitions for parallel consumption.

---

# 17\. Concurrency architecture

Suppose one service receives:

```
10,000 concurrent requests
```

You should ask:

```
How many application threads?
How many DB connections?
How many Kafka consumers?
How many downstream connections?
What is the queue depth?
```

Think of the system as a set of queues:

```
Requests
   │
   ▼
HTTP Thread Pool
   │
   ▼
Application
   │
   ▼
DB Connection Pool
   │
   ▼
Database
```

Every stage has capacity.

If:

```
Incoming = 10,000/sec
DB = 2,000/sec
```

then the DB becomes the bottleneck.

---

# 18\. Little's Law — excellent interview concept

A useful relationship is:

```
L = λ × W
```

Where:

- `L` = average number of concurrent requests
- `λ` = throughput
- `W` = average latency

Example:

```
Throughput = 1,000 requests/sec
Latency = 200 ms = 0.2 sec
```

Then:

```
L = 1000 × 0.2
  = 200 concurrent requests
```

If latency increases to:

```
5 seconds
```

then:

```
L = 1000 × 5
  = 5,000 concurrent requests
```

This explains why latency problems can turn into concurrency problems.

```
Latency ↑
   ↓
Concurrent requests ↑
   ↓
Threads/connections ↑
   ↓
Resource exhaustion
   ↓
Latency ↑
```

That's a very useful production mental model.

---

# 19\. Huge traffic + database architecture

Suppose:

```
100K requests/sec
```

You don't want every request directly hitting one database.

Possible architecture:

```
                   API Gateway
                        │
                        ▼
                  Load Balancer
                        │
             ┌──────────┼──────────┐
             ▼          ▼          ▼
           Service    Service    Service
             │          │          │
             └──────┬───┴──────┬───┘
                    ▼          ▼
                  Cache      Kafka
                    │          │
                    ▼          ▼
                  Read DB    Workers
                               │
                               ▼
                             Write DB
```

---

# 20\. Backpressure

This is an important senior-level concept.

Suppose:

```
Producer = 100K/sec
Consumer = 20K/sec
```

If you simply accept everything:

```
Queue
████████████████████████████████
████████████████████████████████
████████████████████████████████
```

Eventually memory/disk/resources can become exhausted.

Backpressure means:

> **Don't allow a faster producer to overwhelm a slower consumer indefinitely.**

Possible strategies:

```
Rate limiting
Queue limits
Load shedding
Bounded thread pools
Bounded queues
Consumer scaling
Batching
Async processing
```

---

# 21\. Rate limiting

Huge traffic doesn't always mean you should scale infinitely.

Suppose an API can safely handle:

```
10K requests/sec
```

but traffic suddenly becomes:

```
100K requests/sec
```

Use:

```
Client
  │
  ▼
API Gateway
  │
  ├── Rate Limit
  │
  ├── Authentication
  │
  └── Routing
       │
       ▼
    Services
```

Return:

```
HTTP 429 Too Many Requests
```

when appropriate.

This protects the backend.

---

# 22\. Load shedding

During extreme overload:

```
100K requests
      │
      ▼
System capacity = 20K
```

Don't let all requests enter the system and eventually timeout.

Instead:

```
100K requests
      │
      ▼
Admission control
      │
      ├── 20K accepted
      │
      └── 80K rejected/throttled
```

It's often better to return a fast failure than to make the entire platform unavailable.

---

# 23\. Architecture scenario: flash sale

### Interviewer

> An ecommerce flash sale starts at 10 AM. Traffic increases 50x. Design the backend.

Strong architecture:

```
                       Users
                         │
                         ▼
                       CDN
                         │
                         ▼
                  Load Balancer
                         │
                         ▼
                    API Gateway
                         │
             ┌───────────┼───────────┐
             │           │           │
             ▼           ▼           ▼
          Product      Cart        Order
          Service     Service      Service
             │           │           │
             ▼           ▼           ▼
           Cache       Redis       Kafka
             │                       │
             │                       ▼
             │                    Workers
             │                       │
             ▼                       ▼
          Read DB                  Order DB
```

Important techniques:

- Horizontal autoscaling
- CDN
- Cache
- Redis
- Rate limiting
- Queue-based asynchronous processing
- Kafka partitioning
- Idempotency
- Database indexing
- Read replicas
- Load shedding
- Backpressure

---

# 24\. Flash-sale inventory problem

There's another issue.

Suppose:

```
Product stock = 100
```

but:

```
10,000 users
```

simultaneously try to purchase.

You must prevent:

```
stock = -500
```

Potential approaches include:

```
Atomic inventory operation
Distributed locking where justified
Database conditional update
Optimistic concurrency
Redis atomic operations
Queue-based reservation
```

For example:

```
UPDATE inventory
SET available = available - 1
WHERE product_id = ?
AND available > 0;
```

Then check affected rows:

```
1 row → reservation succeeded
0 rows → out of stock
```

This is often safer than:

```
SELECT stock
UPDATE stock
```

without concurrency protection.

---

# 25\. Concurrency scenario: duplicate order

Suppose the client sends:

```
POST /orders
```

twice because the first request timed out.

You might accidentally create:

```
Order 1001
Order 1002
```

for the same purchase.

Use an idempotency key:

```
Idempotency-Key: abc-123
```

Architecture:

```
Client
  │
  ▼
API
  │
  ▼
Idempotency Store
  │
  ├── key exists → return previous result
  │
  └── new key → process request
```

This is critical in high-traffic payment/order systems.

---

# 26\. Backend bottleneck decision tree

When something is slow:

```
                API SLOW
                   │
          ┌────────┴────────┐
          ▼                 ▼
       CPU high           CPU normal
          │                 │
          ▼                 ▼
    Compute bound        I/O bound?
                            │
                  ┌─────────┼─────────┐
                  ▼         ▼         ▼
                 DB       Kafka     HTTP API
                  │         │         │
                  ▼         ▼         ▼
               Query      Lag      Timeout
               Lock      Consumer  Retry
               Pool      Capacity  Circuit
```

When CPU is high:

```
CPU
 │
 ├── Application code
 ├── Serialization
 ├── GC
 ├── Encryption
 └── Excessive traffic
```

When memory is high:

```
Memory
 │
 ├── Leak
 ├── Large objects
 ├── Cache
 ├── Queue
 ├── Excessive concurrency
 └── Large payloads
```

When latency is high:

```
Latency
 │
 ├── CPU
 ├── DB
 ├── Network
 ├── External API
 ├── Lock
 ├── Queue
 ├── GC
 └── Retry
```

---

# 27\. The architecture-level answer pattern

For **any** scenario, give your answer in this order:

### 1\. Detect

```
Metrics
Logs
Traces
Alerts
```

### 2\. Measure

```
RPS
Latency
Error rate
CPU
Memory
Concurrency
Queue depth
DB pool
Kafka lag
```

### 3\. Find the bottleneck

```
CPU?
Memory?
DB?
Kafka?
Network?
External service?
Lock?
```

### 4\. Mitigate

```
Scale
Cache
Rate limit
Circuit breaker
Reduce traffic
Rollback
Load shed
Increase consumers
```

### 5\. Fix architecture

```
Async processing
Caching
Partitioning
Read replicas
Sharding
Queueing
Backpressure
Idempotency
Database optimization
```

### 6\. Validate

```
SLO
Latency
Throughput
Error rate
Resource utilization
```

---

# 28\. A very strong interview example

If the interviewer says:

> **"Your service has 100K requests/sec, p99 latency is 8 seconds, CPU is only 20%, memory is 40%, and DB CPU is 90%. What would you do?"**

Don't say:

> "Scale the service."

Say:

> "The low application CPU combined with high database CPU suggests the database is probably the bottleneck. I'd confirm this with distributed traces, query latency, database CPU, connection-pool metrics, slow-query analysis and lock metrics. Horizontally scaling the application could actually increase database pressure. I'd first optimize the expensive queries and indexes, introduce caching for read-heavy traffic, consider read replicas, reduce unnecessary database calls, and use asynchronous processing for operations that don't need to be synchronous. If database capacity still isn't sufficient, I'd evaluate partitioning or sharding based on the access pattern. I'd also use rate limiting and backpressure to prevent traffic from overwhelming the database."

**That is a much stronger senior-level answer.**

---

# 29\. The one diagram to memorize

```
                         TRAFFIC
                            │
                            ▼
                     Load Balancer
                            │
                            ▼
                      API Gateway
                            │
              ┌─────────────┼─────────────┐
              ▼             ▼             ▼
            Pod 1         Pod 2         Pod 3
              │             │             │
              └─────────────┼─────────────┘
                            │
                ┌───────────┼───────────┐
                ▼           ▼           ▼
              Cache       Kafka         DB
                │           │           │
                │           ▼           │
                │        Workers        │
                │           │           │
                └───────────┼───────────┘
                            ▼
                     External APIs

OBSERVABILITY
────────────────────────────────────────────
Metrics → Prometheus → Grafana → Alerts
Logs   → Loki/ELK    → Grafana
Traces → OTel        → Jaeger
────────────────────────────────────────────
```

Then reason about **capacity at every box**:

```
Traffic
  ↓
API capacity
  ↓
Thread capacity
  ↓
Connection capacity
  ↓
Kafka capacity
  ↓
Database capacity
  ↓
External dependency capacity
```

That is the architecture-level thinking interviewers are looking for.

## Next interview scenario

> **Your service is running 10 pods. Each pod has 4 CPU and 8 GB RAM. Traffic has increased 5x. CPU is now 85%, p95 latency has increased from 300 ms to 2 seconds, but the database CPU is only 30%.**
>
>  **Would you scale vertically or horizontally? What metrics would you check before deciding, and what architecture changes would you make if traffic is expected to increase another 10x?**

---
## Mock Interview — Scaling Scenario

### Interviewer

Your service currently runs **10 pods**:

```
10 pods
4 CPU / pod
8 GB RAM / pod
```

Traffic has increased **5×**.

Current metrics:

```
CPU:          85%
Memory:       55%
p95 latency:  300 ms → 2 sec
p99 latency:  4 sec
DB CPU:       30%
Error rate:   2%
```

Traffic is expected to increase **another 10×** over the next few months.

### Question

**Would you scale vertically or horizontally?**

And explain:

1. What metrics would you check before making the decision?
2. Why might horizontal scaling be preferable?
3. When would vertical scaling make sense?
4. What could go wrong if you simply add more pods?
5. How would you architect the system for another 10× traffic?
6. How would you handle latency, concurrency, caching, database load, Kafka, and downstream services?

Answer as if you're speaking to a **Senior/Lead Architect interviewer**.

### Your target answer structure

Try to answer in this order:

```
1. Understand the bottleneck
        ↓
2. Check CPU / memory / threads
        ↓
3. Check throughput and latency
        ↓
4. Check DB / cache / Kafka / downstreams
        ↓
5. Decide horizontal vs vertical
        ↓
6. Protect dependencies
        ↓
7. Design for 10× growth
```

**Your turn.** Give me your answer, and I'll grade it out of 10 and then show you the ideal Senior Architect answer.

---
## Model Answer — Senior Architect Level

> “I would primarily choose **horizontal scaling**, but I would first validate that CPU saturation is actually the bottleneck and that the application is stateless enough to scale horizontally.
>
>  We currently have 10 pods × 4 CPU, so approximately 40 CPU available. CPU is at 85%, while memory is only 55%. The database is at only 30%, so the immediate bottleneck appears to be the application tier rather than the database.
>
>  I would first check CPU throttling, request rate, requests per pod, thread-pool utilization, GC, network, connection pools, and downstream latency. I would also look at p50, p95 and p99 latency rather than relying only on CPU.”

### 1\. Why horizontal scaling?

I would increase:

```
10 pods
  ↓
20 pods
  ↓
30 pods
```

rather than immediately making each pod:

```
4 CPU
  ↓
16 CPU
```

because horizontal scaling gives us:

- Higher capacity
- Better availability
- Better failure isolation
- Rolling deployment capability
- Easier autoscaling
- Better cloud/Kubernetes utilization

The architecture becomes:

```
                  Load Balancer
                       │
       ┌───────────────┼───────────────┐
       ▼               ▼               ▼
     Pod 1           Pod 2           Pod N
       │               │               │
       └───────────────┼───────────────┘
                       ▼
                 Dependencies
```

---

# 2\. But I wouldn't blindly add pods

This is an important senior-level point.

Before scaling, I'd ask:

> **What happens to downstream dependencies when I multiply application capacity?**

For example:

```
10 pods
×
1,000 DB requests/pod
=
10,000 DB requests
```

If I increase to 100 pods:

```
100 pods
×
1,000 DB requests
=
100,000 DB requests
```

The database may suddenly become the bottleneck.

So I would check:

```
Application
   │
   ├── Database
   ├── Redis
   ├── Kafka
   ├── Payment API
   └── Other microservices
```

and determine the capacity of each dependency.

---

# 3\. Metrics I would check

### Application

```
RPS
p50 / p95 / p99 latency
error rate
CPU
memory
GC
thread pool
request queue
```

### Database

```
CPU
connections
connection-pool utilization
query latency
slow queries
locks
I/O
transactions/sec
```

### Kafka

```
producer throughput
consumer throughput
consumer lag
partition utilization
rebalance frequency
consumer processing time
```

### External services

```
latency
timeouts
error rate
retry count
circuit-breaker state
```

---

# 4\. Why is latency 2 seconds if CPU is 85%?

I'd investigate whether CPU saturation is actually causing the latency.

For example:

```
Request
  │
  ▼
Order Service
  │
  ├── Application processing   200 ms
  ├── DB                       100 ms
  ├── Redis                    20 ms
  └── CPU queue/wait         1,500 ms
```

If threads are waiting for CPU scheduling, increasing pods could help.

But if the trace instead shows:

```
Order Service       300 ms
Payment Service    1,500 ms
Database             100 ms
```

then scaling Order Service alone won't solve the problem.

---

# 5\. Vertical scaling

I would consider vertical scaling when:

- The workload isn't easily horizontally scalable.
- The service has large memory requirements.
- A single process needs more CPU/memory.
- The workload is stateful.
- Horizontal scaling has significant coordination overhead.
- We have reached practical pod/instance limits.

For example:

```
Large in-memory computation

1 pod
16 CPU
64 GB RAM
```

might make sense.

But I would avoid using vertical scaling as the only long-term strategy for a highly scalable stateless API.

---

# 6\. Autoscaling

For this scenario I'd consider Kubernetes HPA.

Conceptually:

```
minReplicas: 10
maxReplicas: 100

targetCPUUtilization: 60
```

The important point is that I wouldn't blindly use CPU alone.

For this application, I might also consider:

```
CPU
+
RPS per pod
+
request latency
+
queue depth
```

For asynchronous services, queue depth or Kafka lag can be a better scaling signal than CPU.

---

# 7\. Design for another 10× traffic

If traffic is going from:

```
Current
  ↓
5× increase
  ↓
another 10×
```

I would design for horizontal scalability from the beginning.

Architecture:

```
                       Users
                         │
                         ▼
                        CDN
                         │
                         ▼
                  Load Balancer
                         │
                         ▼
                    API Gateway
                         │
              ┌──────────┼──────────┐
              ▼          ▼          ▼
           Pod 1       Pod 2      Pod N
              │          │          │
              └──────────┼──────────┘
                         │
            ┌────────────┼────────────┐
            ▼            ▼            ▼
          Redis         Kafka        DB
            │            │            │
            │            ▼            │
            │         Workers         │
            │            │            │
            └────────────┼────────────┘
                         ▼
                  External Services
```

---

# 8\. Make the service stateless

For horizontal scaling, I want:

```
Pod 1
Pod 2
Pod 3
Pod 4
```

to be interchangeable.

Avoid storing important session state inside:

```
static Map<String, UserSession>
```

or local memory.

Instead:

```
Application
    │
    ├── Redis
    ├── Database
    └── Object Storage
```

Then any request can go to any pod.

---

# 9\. Caching

If traffic increases 10× and the application repeatedly reads the same data, I would introduce caching.

For example:

```
GET /products/123
          │
          ▼
        Redis
          │
     ┌────┴────┐
     │         │
   HIT        MISS
     │         │
     ▼         ▼
 Response      DB
                 │
                 ▼
               Redis
```

This can dramatically reduce database load.

But I'd consider:

- TTL
- cache invalidation
- cache stampede
- cache consistency
- memory limits
- hot keys

---

# 10\. Cache stampede

Imagine a popular product expires from cache.

At exactly the same time:

```
10,000 requests
      │
      ▼
Cache MISS
      │
      ▼
10,000 DB queries
```

That's dangerous.

Possible solutions:

```
Request coalescing
Distributed locking
Refresh-ahead
Jittered TTL
Background refresh
```

This is a good architecture-level detail to mention.

---

# 11\. Database strategy

Since DB CPU is currently only 30%, I wouldn't immediately introduce sharding.

But for another 10× traffic, I'd evaluate:

```
Indexes
Query optimization
Connection pool
Read replicas
Caching
Partitioning
Archival
Sharding
```

For read-heavy workloads:

```
                 Application
                     │
                     ▼
                   Cache
                     │
                     ▼
                Read Replica
                 /        \
                /          \
          Replica 1      Replica 2
```

For very large datasets, partitioning may become important.

---

# 12\. Kafka for traffic smoothing

If some operations don't need to happen synchronously, I'd move them to asynchronous processing.

Instead of:

```
HTTP
 ↓
Order
 ↓
Inventory
 ↓
Payment
 ↓
Email
 ↓
Analytics
 ↓
Response
```

I'd keep the critical path short:

```
HTTP
 ↓
Order
 ↓
Commit
 ↓
Response
```

Then:

```
             Kafka
               │
       ┌───────┼────────┐
       ▼       ▼        ▼
 Inventory   Email   Analytics
```

This improves resilience and allows consumers to scale independently.

---

# 13\. Backpressure

With 10× traffic, I would explicitly design for backpressure.

Suppose:

```
Incoming = 100K/sec
Processing = 30K/sec
```

Don't allow unlimited requests into the system.

Use:

```
Rate limiting
     ↓
Bounded queues
     ↓
Kafka
     ↓
Consumer scaling
     ↓
Load shedding
```

If necessary:

```
429 Too Many Requests
```

is preferable to allowing the entire platform to collapse.

---

# 14\. Concurrency control

I'd also check:

```
Tomcat/Netty threads
DB connections
HTTP client connections
Kafka consumers
Redis connections
```

For example:

```
10,000 concurrent requests
       │
       ▼
200 HTTP threads
       │
       ▼
20 DB connections
```

If requests block on the database, adding more application pods may only increase pressure.

---

# 15\. Protect downstream services

For a 10× traffic increase, I'd use:

```
Timeouts
Retries with limits
Circuit breakers
Bulkheads
Rate limits
Connection limits
```

Example:

```
Order Service
      │
      ▼
Payment Service
      │
      ▼
External Provider
```

If Payment becomes unhealthy:

```
Order
  │
  ▼
Circuit Breaker OPEN
  │
  X
Payment
```

This prevents cascading failures.

---

# 16\. Bulkhead pattern

Separate resources for different workloads.

For example:

```
                    Order Service
                         │
              ┌──────────┴──────────┐
              ▼                     ▼
        Payment pool           Inventory pool
        50 connections         50 connections
```

If Payment becomes slow, it shouldn't consume every thread/connection and prevent Inventory operations.

---

# 17\. Final architecture answer

My complete answer to the interviewer would be:

> **“I would favor horizontal scaling because this appears to be a stateless service with CPU utilization at 85%, while memory and database utilization are relatively low. However, before scaling I would validate the bottleneck using request rate, p95/p99 latency, CPU throttling, thread-pool utilization, GC, connection pools, distributed traces and downstream latency.**
>
>  **I would scale the service horizontally using autoscaling rather than simply increasing the size of each instance. But I would also verify that downstream systems can handle the additional traffic because adding application pods can amplify database, Kafka and external-service load.**
>
>  **For the expected 10× growth, I'd design the service to be stateless, use load balancing and autoscaling, introduce caching for read-heavy workloads, use Kafka for asynchronous processing, optimize database access and potentially use read replicas or partitioning as data volume grows.**
>
>  **I'd also introduce rate limiting, backpressure, timeouts, bounded retries, circuit breakers and bulkheads to prevent cascading failures. Finally, I'd define SLOs around availability and latency and use metrics, logs and distributed tracing to continuously validate capacity.”**

### The senior-level takeaway

Don't answer:

> **“CPU is high, so add more servers.”**

Answer:

> **“Identify the bottleneck → determine whether the workload is horizontally scalable → scale the bottleneck → verify downstream capacity → protect dependencies → design for future traffic.”**

That distinction is what separates a **developer-level scaling answer** from a **Senior/Lead Architect answer**.

---
# Part 9 — Mock Interview: Memory, GC & Concurrency

### Interviewer

Your **Spring Boot Order Service** is running in production with:

```
20 pods
8 CPU / pod
16 GB RAM / pod
```

Suddenly you observe:

```
Heap usage:       45% → 92%
Old Gen:          40% → 88%
GC frequency:     increasing
GC pause:         100 ms → 3 seconds
p95 latency:      300 ms → 4 seconds
CPU:              70%
DB CPU:           35%
HTTP traffic:     normal
Kafka traffic:    normal
```

After several minutes, some pods restart with:

```
java.lang.OutOfMemoryError: Java heap space
```

At the same time, the number of concurrent requests has increased significantly, even though incoming RPS hasn't changed much.

## Your questions

Explain how you would investigate this incident.

Specifically cover:

1. **How do you determine whether this is a memory leak or simply high memory pressure?**
2. **How do GC pauses cause API latency?**
3. **Why can concurrency increase even when RPS remains constant?**
4. **Would you increase the JVM heap?**
5. **Would you increase the number of pods?**
6. **What metrics, JVM tools, and thread information would you collect?**
7. **How would you identify the offending code?**
8. **What architectural changes could prevent this problem at 10× traffic?**

### Think about this relationship

```
Latency ↑
    │
    ▼
Requests remain active longer
    │
    ▼
Concurrency ↑
    │
    ▼
More objects / buffers / threads
    │
    ▼
Heap pressure ↑
    │
    ▼
GC ↑
    │
    ▼
GC pauses ↑
    │
    ▼
Latency ↑
```

This is a **very important Senior-level production scenario** because the root cause may not be simply "we need more memory."

Give me your answer as if you're in the interview.

---
# Model Answer — Memory, GC & Con JVM memory and latency incident, but I wouldn't immediately increase the heap. First I would establish whether we're dealing with a memory leak, excessive allocationcurrency

> “I would treat this as a JVM memory and latency incident, but I wouldn't immediately increase the heap. First I would establish whether we're dealing with a memory leak, excessive allocation, increased concurrency, or simply insufficient heap for the workload.”

## 1\. First, establish the scope

I'd compare healthy and unhealthy pods:

```
                    Healthy       Unhealthy
Heap                  45%            92%
Old Gen               40%            88%
GC pause              100ms          3s
CPU                    50%            70%
RPS                   normal         normal
```

I'd determine:

- Did all pods exhibit the problem?
- Did it begin after a deployment?
- Is one endpoint responsible?
- Did request/response payload sizes increase?
- Did concurrency increase?
- Did JVM configuration change?

I'd correlate the start of the incident with deployments and traffic patterns.

---

# 2\. Memory leak vs high allocation pressure

This is one of the most important distinctions.

### Healthy/high allocation

```
Heap
 │
 │    /\      /\      /\
 │   /  \    /  \    /  \
 │__/____\__/____\__/____\__
       GC      GC      GC
```

GC successfully removes temporary objects.

### Potential memory leak

```
Heap
 │
 │          /\
 │        /    \__
 │      /          \__
 │    /               \__
 │___/____________________
```

The key question is:

> **What is the heap occupancy after GC?**

If:

```
Before GC = 90%
After GC  = 45%
```

that's very different from:

```
Before GC = 90%
After GC  = 85%
```

If the post-GC baseline continually increases, I'd strongly suspect retained objects/a memory leak.

---

# 3\. JVM metrics I'd inspect

I'd monitor:

```
Heap used
Heap committed
Heap max
Eden usage
Old Gen usage
Allocation rate
GC count
GC pause duration
Full GC count
Thread count
Class count
Direct/off-heap memory
```

I'd also look at:

```
process_resident_memory
jvm_memory_used
jvm_memory_max
jvm_gc_pause
jvm_threads_live
```

through Micrometer/Prometheus.

---

# 4\. Capture a heap dump

If the issue is reproducible and operationally safe:

```
jcmd <PID> GC.heap_dump /tmp/order-service.hprof
```

Then analyze it with a heap-analysis tool.

I'd look for:

```
Large retained objects
Large HashMaps
Large Lists
Caches
Static references
ThreadLocal references
Large byte[]
Large JSON objects
Unbounded queues
Kafka payloads
```

The important concept is **retained size**, not simply object count.

---

# 5\. Example root cause

Suppose we discover:

```
private static final Map<String, Order> CACHE =
        new HashMap<>();
```

and entries are never removed.

Then:

```
Requests
   ↓
Orders
   ↓
CACHE
   ↓
Objects remain reachable
   ↓
GC cannot reclaim them
   ↓
Old Gen grows
   ↓
Full GC
   ↓
OOM
```

The fix isn't:

```
-Xmx32g
```

The fix is:

```
Bounded cache
+
TTL
+
Eviction
```

or move the cache to an appropriate external caching system.

---

# 6\. Why does GC cause latency?

Suppose the JVM has:

```
Heap = 16 GB
```

and becomes heavily occupied.

GC starts working harder:

```
Application
    │
    ▼
Allocation
    │
    ▼
Heap pressure
    │
    ▼
GC
    │
    ▼
Pause
```

If GC pauses application execution for 3 seconds:

```
Request
   │
   ▼
Application
   │
   X
  GC pause
   │
   ▼
Response
```

That request's latency increases by approximately the pause duration, depending on the collector and execution path.

As GC pressure grows:

```
GC ↑
 ↓
Pause ↑
 ↓
Latency ↑
 ↓
Concurrent requests ↑
```

---

# 7\. Why can concurrency increase when RPS is unchanged?

This is where **Little's Law** is useful.

```
L = λ × W
```

Where:

```
L = concurrency
λ = throughput
W = latency
```

Suppose:

```
RPS = 1,000
Latency = 200 ms
```

Then approximately:

```
L = 1000 × 0.2
  = 200 concurrent requests
```

Now latency becomes 4 seconds:

```
L = 1000 × 4
  = 4,000 concurrent requests
```

**Same traffic. 20× more concurrent work.**

That's why your observation is important:

```
RPS normal
Latency ↑
        ↓
Concurrency ↑
        ↓
Memory/object usage ↑
```

This can create a positive feedback loop.

---

# 8\. The dangerous feedback loop

```
           GC pressure
                │
                ▼
          Latency increases
                │
                ▼
       Requests stay active longer
                │
                ▼
        Concurrency increases
                │
                ▼
      More objects/resources
                │
                ▼
          Heap pressure
                │
                └──────────► GC pressure
```

This is potentially a cascading resource failure **inside one service**.

---

# 9\. Would I increase the JVM heap?

**Not immediately.**

Increasing:

```
-Xmx16g
      ↓
-Xmx32g
```

might temporarily delay OOM.

But if there is a leak:

```
16 GB → OOM
32 GB → OOM later
64 GB → OOM even later
```

So I'd first establish the cause.

I might increase heap temporarily as a mitigation if there is enough host/container memory, but I wouldn't consider that the permanent fix.

---

# 10\. Would I increase the number of pods?

Potentially, but again not blindly.

If the problem is:

```
CPU saturation
+
legitimate high workload
+
stateless service
```

then horizontal scaling can help:

```
20 pods
 ↓
40 pods
```

But if every request creates excessive objects because of a memory leak:

```
20 pods → memory leak
40 pods → memory leak
```

you haven't fixed the root cause.

You've doubled the number of leaking processes.

---

# 11\. I'd investigate concurrency limits

I'd inspect:

```
HTTP thread pool
HTTP client connection pool
DB connection pool
Kafka consumer concurrency
ExecutorService pools
Queue sizes
```

Suppose:

```
HTTP threads = 500
DB connections = 50
```

and requests frequently need DB access.

You could have:

```
500 HTTP requests
       │
       ▼
50 DB connections
       │
       ▼
450 requests waiting
```

Those waiting requests still consume resources.

---

# 12\. Check thread dumps

I'd use:

```
jcmd <PID> Thread.print
```

or:

```
jstack <PID>
```

I'd look for:

```
BLOCKED
WAITING
TIMED_WAITING
RUNNABLE
```

and determine whether many threads are:

- waiting on locks
- waiting for database connections
- waiting for external APIs
- waiting on queues
- consuming CPU

This helps distinguish a memory problem from a concurrency/locking problem.

---

# 13\. Example thread problem

Suppose traces show:

```
Payment API = 8 seconds
```

and 500 request threads are waiting for Payment.

Then:

```
500 threads
   ↓
External API
   ↓
8-second latency
```

Eventually:

```
Thread pool exhausted
       ↓
Requests queue
       ↓
Latency increases
       ↓
Concurrency increases
       ↓
Memory pressure increases
```

The real solution might be:

```
Timeout
Circuit breaker
Bulkhead
Async processing
Bounded concurrency
```

—not simply increasing heap.

---

# 14\. Check allocation rate

A memory problem isn't always a leak.

Suppose every request creates:

```
Large JSON
Large DTO
Temporary byte arrays
Serialization buffers
```

At:

```
10,000 requests/sec
```

the JVM may allocate enormous amounts of temporary data.

Then:

```
Allocation rate ↑
      ↓
Young GC ↑
      ↓
CPU ↑
      ↓
GC pressure ↑
```

In that case, I'd optimize allocations rather than hunt for a retained-object leak.

---

# 15\. Large payload problem

Imagine:

```
Request = 1 MB
Response = 5 MB
```

At:

```
10,000 requests/sec
```

you're potentially moving huge amounts of data through the application.

I'd consider:

```
Pagination
Streaming
Compression
Smaller DTOs
Field selection
Maximum payload size
Async bulk processing
Object storage for large files
```

Don't allow unlimited payload sizes.

---

# 16\. Architecture for 10× traffic

I'd design the service around bounded resource usage:

```
                    Load Balancer
                         │
              ┌──────────┼──────────┐
              ▼          ▼          ▼
            Pod 1      Pod 2      Pod N
              │          │          │
              └──────────┼──────────┘
                         │
             ┌───────────┼───────────┐
             ▼           ▼           ▼
           Redis       Kafka         DB
             │           │           │
             │           ▼           │
             │        Workers        │
             │                       │
             └───────────────────────┘
```

And I'd enforce:

```
Bounded thread pools
Bounded queues
Bounded caches
Connection limits
Request size limits
Timeouts
Rate limits
Backpressure
Circuit breakers
```

The word **bounded** is extremely important in high-scale systems.

---

# 17\. Move long-running work out of HTTP

If an operation takes several seconds:

Instead of:

```
POST /orders
   ↓
Do everything
   ↓
Wait 10 sec
   ↓
Response
```

when business requirements allow it:

```
POST /orders
      │
      ▼
Create order
      │
      ▼
Kafka
      │
      ▼
202 Accepted
```

Then:

```
Kafka
 │
 ├── Inventory Worker
 ├── Payment Worker
 ├── Notification Worker
 └── Analytics Worker
```

This limits mitigate safely, and then change the architecture so the same failure doesn't happen at 10× scale synchronous request concurrency.

---

# 18\. Prevent memory problems architecturally

I would specifically look for:

```
❌ Unbounded cache
❌ Unbounded queue
❌ Unlimited concurrency
❌ Huge HTTP payloads
❌ Huge Kafka messages
❌ Loading millions of DB rows into memory
❌ Long-lived transactions
❌ Unbounded thread creation
```

Replace them with:

```
✅ Bounded cache
✅ Bounded queue
✅ Concurrency limits
✅ Pagination/streaming
✅ Message-size limits
✅ Batch processing
✅ Short transactions
✅ Fixed/bounded executors
```

---

# 19\. My final interview answer

If I had to answer the interviewer in 60–90 seconds:

> **“I would first determine whether this is a memory leak, excessive allocation, or increased concurrency caused by latency. The key metric I'd look at is post-GC heap occupancy. If the heap remains high after GC and the baseline continuously increases, I'd capture a heap dump and inspect retained objects, dominators, caches, collections and ThreadLocal references.**
>
>  **I'd also investigate GC pause time because a 3-second pause can directly increase request latency. Since RPS is normal but concurrency has increased, I'd apply Little's Law: concurrency equals throughput multiplied by latency. At the same throughput, increasing latency from 200 ms to 4 seconds can increase concurrent requests by roughly 20×.**
>
>  **I'd inspect thread pools, DB and HTTP connection pools, queue depth, thread dumps and distributed traces to determine whether requests are blocked on dependencies.**
>
>  **I wouldn't immediately increase the heap because that can only delay an OOM if there's a leak. I also wouldn't blindly add pods unless the workload is legitimately CPU-bound and the service is horizontally scalable.**
>
>  **For the long-term architecture, I'd use bounded concurrency, bounded queues and caches, request-size limits, pagination, streaming, caching, asynchronous Kafka-based processing, timeouts, circuit breakers and backpressure. The goal is not simply to add resources but to prevent unbounded work from entering the system.”**

### What the interviewer is really testing

They aren't just testing whether you know **JVM memory**.

They're testing whether you understand this:

```
                TRAFFIC
                   │
                   ▼
               THROUGHPUT
                   │
                   ▼
                LATENCY
                   │
                   ▼
              CONCURRENCY
                   │
          ┌────────┼────────┐
          ▼        ▼        ▼
         CPU     MEMORY      I/O
          │        │        │
          └────────┼────────┘
                   ▼
              BOTTLENECK
                   │
                   ▼
          ARCHITECTURE DECISION
```

That is the **Senior/Lead backend engineer mindset**: identify the limiting resource, prove it with observability, mitigate safely, and then change the architecture so the same failure doesn't happen at 10× scale.

---
# Part 10 — Database Connection Pool Exhaustion

### Interviewer

Your **Order Service** has 30 Spring Boot pods.

Each pod has:

```
HikariCP maximumPoolSize = 30
```

So the theoretical maximum is:

```
30 pods × 30 connections = 900 DB connections
```

Suddenly production shows:

```
API p95:              400 ms → 8 sec
API p99:              12 sec
CPU:                  35%
Memory:               50%
DB CPU:               65%
Active DB connections: 900
Pending DB requests:   very high
DB query latency:      100 ms → 2 sec
HTTP traffic:          3× normal
Error rate:            increasing
```

Application logs contain:

```
Connection is not available, request timed out after 30000ms
```

## Your task

Explain how you would investigate and solve this **architecture-level problem**.

Cover these areas:

1. Why can **900 database connections** actually make the system slower?
2. How do you distinguish **connection-pool exhaustion** from a slow database?
3. What HikariCP metrics would you monitor?
4. Would you increase `maximumPoolSize` from 30 to 60?
5. How does connection-pool sizing relate to **number of pods**?
6. How can one slow SQL query cause an entire microservice to become unavailable?
7. How would you use **timeouts, bulkheads and backpressure** here?
8. Would you introduce **read replicas, caching, Kafka, or database sharding**?
9. What happens if you horizontally scale from 30 pods to 100 pods?
10. Design a robust database architecture for **10× traffic**.

### Important numbers

Think carefully about:

```
30 pods × 30 connections = 900
```

If you scale to 100 pods:

```
100 × 30 = 3,000 DB connections
```

That may **make the problem dramatically worse**.

Your answer should demonstrate that you understand the difference between:

> **Application capacity**

and

> **Database capacity**

and why blindly increasing either one can cause a cascading failure.

**Answer as if you're in a Senior Java Microservices / Architect interview.**

---
# Model Answer — Database Connection Pool Exhaustion

This is a very important **Senior Microservices interview scenario** because the obvious answer—**“increase the connection pool”**—can actually make the outage worse.

## 1\. Start with the symptoms

We have:

```
30 pods
30 DB connections/pod
= 900 possible DB connections

API p95        400 ms → 8 sec
CPU            35%
Memory         50%
DB CPU         65%
DB query       100 ms → 2 sec
Active conns   900
Pending        HIGH
```

My first conclusion would be:

> **The application isn't CPU-bound. Requests are probably waiting on database connections and/or slow database operations.**

I'd verify that with metrics and distributed traces before changing configuration.

---

# 2\. How does connection-pool exhaustion happen?

Normally:

```
HTTP Request
     │
     ▼
Service
     │
     ▼
HikariCP
     │
     ▼
DB Connection
     │
     ▼
Database
```

Suppose all 30 connections are busy:

```
HikariCP

Connection 1 ── busy
Connection 2 ── busy
Connection 3 ── busy
...
Connection 30 ── busy
```

A new request arrives:

```
Request
   │
   ▼
No connection available
   │
   ▼
WAIT
   │
   ▼
Timeout
```

Eventually:

```
Latency ↑
   ↓
Requests remain active longer
   ↓
Concurrency ↑
   ↓
More connection demand
   ↓
Pool exhausted
   ↓
Timeouts
   ↓
Errors
```

---

# 3\. Why can 900 connections make the database slower?

This is the key architecture concept.

More connections do **not** automatically mean more throughput.

Imagine the database can efficiently process:

```
500 concurrent operations
```

but we give it:

```
900 connections
```

Now potentially hundreds of operations compete for:

- CPU
- memory
- locks
- buffer/cache
- disk I/O
- network
- internal database workers

You can get:

```
More connections
      ↓
More concurrency
      ↓
More contention
      ↓
Queries become slower
      ↓
Connections remain occupied longer
      ↓
Pool exhaustion
```

So:

> **A connection pool is a concurrency-control mechanism, not simply a performance booster.**

---

# 4\. How do I distinguish pool exhaustion from database slowness?

I'd look at both application and database metrics.

### Case A — Pool exhaustion

You might see:

```
Active connections     = 30/30
Idle connections       = 0
Pending threads        = high
Connection acquisition = high
DB query execution     = relatively normal
```

The application is spending time **waiting for a connection**.

Distributed trace might look like:

```
HTTP request
│
├── Hikari connection acquisition
│       └── 4 seconds
│
└── DB query
        └── 100 ms
```

That's pool contention.

---

### Case B — Database is slow

You might see:

```
Active connections = 30/30
DB query latency   = 4 seconds
DB CPU              = 95%
```

Trace:

```
HTTP request
│
└── DB query
       └── 4 seconds
```

The pool is full **because the database operations themselves are slow**.

This distinction is critical.

---

# 5\. HikariCP metrics I'd monitor

With Spring Boot \+ Micrometer, I'd monitor:

```
hikaricp.connections.active
hikaricp.connections.idle
hikaricp.connections.pending
hikaricp.connections.max
hikaricp.connections.min
hikaricp.connections.acquire
hikaricp.connections.usage
```

The most important relationship is:

```
Active ≈ Max
+
Pending increasing
```

That is a strong indication of pool pressure.

I'd also correlate:

```
DB query latency
DB CPU
DB locks
DB I/O
connection acquisition time
```

---

# 6\. Would I change this?

Current:

```
spring:
  datasource:
    hikari:
      maximum-pool-size: 30
```

My first reaction is **not**:

```
maximum-pool-size: 60
```

because:

```
30 pods × 60
=
1,800 DB connections
```

Instead of:

```
900
```

We've doubled database concurrency.

If the database is already struggling, this could make the incident worse.

---

# 7\. What determines connection-pool size?

A pool should be based on:

```
Database capacity
+
Query execution time
+
Expected throughput
+
Application concurrency
+
Number of application instances
```

For example:

```
30 pods
×
20 connections
=
600 maximum connections
```

isn't necessarily better or worse than 900.

The correct number depends on what the database can actually process efficiently.

---

# 8\. Why can one slow query cause an outage?

Imagine:

```
Pool = 30 connections
```

One query normally takes:

```
100 ms
```

But a bad query starts taking:

```
10 seconds
```

If enough requests execute that query:

```
Connection 1 → slow query
Connection 2 → slow query
...
Connection 30 → slow query
```

Now:

```
30/30 connections busy
```

New requests cannot obtain connections.

Then:

```
API
 ↓
Wait for connection
 ↓
Timeout
 ↓
Retry
 ↓
More requests
 ↓
More pressure
```

This can become a cascading failure.

---

# 9\. Retries can make it much worse

Suppose a request fails after 5 seconds.

The client retries:

```
Original request
     │
     ▼
DB wait
     │
   timeout
     │
     ▼
Retry
     │
     ▼
DB wait
```

One user request can now create multiple backend operations.

This creates:

> **Retry amplification**

Architecture:

```
Traffic
  │
  ▼
Service
  │
  ├── Request 1 ── DB
  ├── Retry 1  ─── DB
  ├── Retry 2  ─── DB
  └── Retry 3  ─── DB
```

So I'd use carefully designed:

- Timeouts
- Limited retries
- Exponential backoff
- Jitter
- Circuit breakers

---

# 10\. Timeout strategy

I'd establish timeouts at multiple layers.

For example:

```
HTTP timeout
     ↓
Service timeout
     ↓
DB acquisition timeout
     ↓
DB query timeout
```

The exact values depend on the application's SLO, but the principle is:

> **Never allow a request to wait indefinitely for a scarce resource.**

For example:

```
Connection acquisition timeout = 1–2 sec
```

might be preferable to:

```
30 seconds
```

during severe contention.

The goal is controlled failure rather than resource exhaustion.

---

# 11\. Bulkhead pattern

Suppose the Order Service has:

```
Order queries
Payment queries
Product queries
Reporting queries
```

If all use the same pool:

```
                 DB Pool
                   │
       ┌───────────┼───────────┐
       ▼           ▼           ▼
     Order       Payment     Reports
```

A reporting query could consume the entire pool.

Instead, isolate workloads where appropriate:

```
Order traffic
     │
     ▼
Order resource pool

Reporting traffic
     │
     ▼
Reporting resource pool
```

This is the **bulkhead pattern**.

A failure in one workload doesn't consume every resource.

---

# 12\. Backpressure

If the database can safely process:

```
5,000 operations/sec
```

but the application receives:

```
20,000 operations/sec
```

we shouldn't allow unlimited work into the database.

Use:

```
                    Traffic
                       │
                       ▼
                 Rate Limiting
                       │
                       ▼
                Bounded Queue
                       │
                       ▼
                    Service
                       │
                       ▼
                      DB
```

Some traffic may need to receive:

```
429 Too Many Requests
```

or be processed asynchronously.

It's better than allowing the database and application to collapse.

---

# 13\. Would I use caching?

Absolutely for suitable **read-heavy** operations.

Suppose:

```
GET /product/123
```

is called:

```
50,000 times/sec
```

but the product changes only occasionally.

Instead of:

```
50,000 DB queries/sec
```

use:

```
Request
   │
   ▼
Redis
   │
   ├── HIT ──► Response
   │
   └── MISS
         │
         ▼
        DB
         │
         ▼
       Redis
```

This can dramatically reduce database pressure.

But I'd consider cache consistency, TTL, eviction and cache stampede.

---

# 14\. Would I use read replicas?

If workload is read-heavy:

```
                Application
                    │
              ┌─────┴─────┐
              ▼           ▼
           Primary     Read Replica
              │           │
           Writes        Reads
```

For very high read traffic:

```
                 ┌── Replica 1
                 │
Application ─────┼── Replica 2
                 │
                 └── Replica 3
```

But I'd ensure the application's consistency requirements permit replicas.

---

# 15\. Would I use Kafka?

Kafka doesn't directly solve synchronous DB queries.

But it is useful when the operation doesn't need to complete inside the HTTP request.

Instead of:

```
POST /orders
 ↓
Insert order
 ↓
Send email
 ↓
Update analytics
 ↓
Notify warehouse
 ↓
Response
```

use:

```
POST /orders
      │
      ▼
Create order
      │
      ▼
Kafka
      │
      ▼
202 / success
```

Then:

```
Kafka
 │
 ├── Notification Consumer
 ├── Analytics Consumer
 ├── Warehouse Consumer
 └── Inventory Consumer
```

This reduces synchronous DB/application concurrency.

---

# 16\. What about database sharding?

I would **not jump directly to sharding**.

I'd consider it only after exhausting simpler approaches:

```
1. Query optimization
2. Indexing
3. Connection management
4. Caching
5. Read replicas
6. Partitioning
7. Archival
8. Data-model improvements
9. Then potentially sharding
```

Sharding introduces significant complexity:

```
Routing
Rebalancing
Cross-shard queries
Transactions
Consistency
Operational complexity
```

It's a scalability tool, not the first response to pool exhaustion.

---

# 17\. What happens if we scale from 30 to 100 pods?

This is the trap in the question.

Current:

```
30 pods × 30 connections
= 900
```

After scaling:

```
100 pods × 30 connections
= 3,000
```

Now imagine the database can comfortably handle only:

```
1,000 concurrent connections
```

We have potentially created:

```
3× more database pressure
```

So:

> **Horizontal application scaling without database capacity planning can amplify the bottleneck.**

This is why every microservice architecture needs capacity planning across the entire dependency chain.

---

# 18\. Architecture for 10× traffic

I'd aim for something like:

```
                         Clients
                            │
                            ▼
                         CDN/WAF
                            │
                            ▼
                      API Gateway
                            │
                     Rate Limiting
                            │
                            ▼
                    Load Balancer
                            │
              ┌─────────────┼─────────────┐
              ▼             ▼             ▼
           Order Pod     Order Pod     Order Pod
              │             │             │
              └─────────────┼─────────────┘
                            │
                  ┌─────────┴─────────┐
                  ▼                   ▼
                Redis               Kafka
```
